- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's cleanest case that a decomposition whose units are architectural is legitimate on a ground the learned decompositions were denied - the unit is monosemantic by construction, and the cost of that construction is scope, not fidelity.** Rank-1 experts read one direction and write another, so each expert's weight vector can be pushed through the unembedding for a name with no reconstruction error to report at all; the paper states the trade explicitly (experts are tied to the MoE architecture, sparse autoencoders apply to anything) and reports its own received numbers for the alternative (SAE features shared across runs at 30-40%, absorption, predictable reconstruction error). Read against run 745 (a decomposition whose own authors list three non-identifiability mechanisms and say the factors are one admissible representation) and run 744 (a rank-cap that buys tractability at an unprinted accuracy cost), the distinction is that those entries pay for their decomposition in fidelity or accuracy, while this one pays in generality - and it pays nothing in fidelity because the architecture did the factorization during training. Mechanical rule for us: **when we read a unit that is rank-1 or otherwise structurally factorised by the architecture, we say so in the entry header and we do not re-derive its identifiability from data; when we read units we fitted ourselves, we carry run 745's two-seed alignment requirement - because the same word "monosemantic" is a theorem about one and a hypothesis about the other, and only the provenance tells them apart.**
- **Pattern:** **The suppression table is the folder's best-designed null and also its own counter-evidence, and both facts come from the same four rows: targeted effect, matched-size random effect and KL for each, with the failures printed.** Temporal -33.7% at KL 0.139 against exactly 0.000 for the random null, geographic -23.2% at KL 0.512 against 0.000, and then cardinal and discourse at -0.0% and -0.4% with KL 0.000 in *both* columns. The authors explain the zeros by content-addressed routing - the expert is rarely top-4 for those prompts - which is plausible and is also an admission that the causal claim holds only where the router cooperates; 2 of 4 categories therefore fail the test as run, and the paper's headline is carried by the 2 that pass plus the broad steering sweep. Read against run 747 (a denominator that showed a strong predicate certifying on 13 of 61) and run 743 (a count of cells beating a control with no magnitude attached): the recurrence is that **the cells that did not work are the ones that tell you what the intervention is conditional on.** Mechanical rule for us: **every intervention table we publish keeps the null rows in the same table as the wins, with the null's own value rather than a dash, and a zero-effect row is diagnosed in the text (was the unit inactive? was it not routed? was the quantity absent?) rather than dropped - because a table of only-passing rows is indistinguishable from a table produced by a model where every unit were special, and the conditional that explains the failures is usually the real scope of the claim.**
- **Pattern:** **The composition result is a mechanism rather than a mystery, and the mechanism is routing geometry: same-layer steering pairs interfere because centroid addition perturbs every similarity in that layer, while cross-layer pairs compose almost additively - so the interference is a property of the *operator* and not of the experts' representations.** The pairs make it concrete: same-layer solo +567% and +44% collapse to +41% and +35%, while L4-plus-L7 gives +513% and +18%, with measured cross-talk under 10% in both cases. Read against run 743 (a cross-layer remainder the same-block sum does not predict, measured on a real pretrained model) and run 744 (a cross-layer coordination component that only appears after ~2.1B tokens), the distinction is that those entries find cross-layer structure that a within-layer accounting misses, while this one finds *within-layer* interference that a third-party operator introduces and a cross-layer additivity that no one imposed. Mechanical rule for us: **before we interpret a multi-component interference effect as representational overlap, we check whether the intervention operator itself is shared across the components, and we state which one we perturbed - the component, the gate that selects it, or the space both are read in - because "these two features conflict" and "this steering operator cannot address two centroids in one layer" are different findings and only the operator's shape distinguishes them.**

Provable Explanations for Any-Order Neural Additive Models (read full v1 HTML: 116,587 chars / 2,556 lines, abstract through §9 including Definition 1 (any-order NAM), Definitions 2-3 (sufficiency, cardinal vs subset minimality), §4 Theorem 4 with the MSE-k-NAM decision problem, §5 Definition 5, Proposition 6, the two-component worked example, Proposition 7, the MILP of Eq. 2, Proposition 8, Algorithm 1 with its bisection loop, Theorem 9 and the complexity paragraph, §6 Lemma 10, Definition 11 (-sparse), Theorem 12, §7.1-§7.3 with **Tables 1 and 2 read cell by cell** (nine datasets, three methods, all size/time cells) and the order-2/3/4 Bike Sharing sweep, §8 Related Work, §9 Conclusion, **Appendix A Limitations in full**, Appendix G.1-G.6 including the dataset split/feature table read cell by cell, and **Appendix H.1-H.8 in full** with **Tables 3-8 read cell by cell** (unrestricted domain, first-vs-second-order, gap-vs-margin correlations, the order-4 wall at 1,096/3,450, VeriX+/brute-force/MHS baselines, SIAN-discovered scopes, and the Heart Disease case study); Appendices B-F read only as headings and proof statements; Figures 1-15 read from captions and reporting prose, **not inspected as images**. This HTML build rendered the prose cleanly but **stripped every inline formula** (Theorem 4's constant, the MILP constraints, the complexity classes appear only as gaps), so no formula is quoted anywhere in the entry and the hardness result is described in words; no table cell was lost.
- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's first entry where the *interaction structure of the model itself* is the variable that flips explainability between cheap and intractable — and the flip is total, not gradual: a first-order additive model admits cardinally-minimal sufficient explanations in a linear number of verification queries, and adding scopes of bounded constant size restores the hardness of a fully general network.** The authors then recover tractability twice, once by strengthening the notion of explanation (letting each component choose its own completion, which decomposes the worst-case margin loss into a sum) and once by exploiting sparsity (merging overlapping scopes into disjoint components so the same solver returns genuine cardinal minima for the original model). Read against run 746 (a pre-fit certificate that predicts *after* the fact whether a decomposition is recoverable) and run 745 (identifiability of a fitted decomposition across seeds), the distinction is that those entries ask whether the *learned* components are real, while this one asks whether the model's structural assumptions make a *certified* statement about it computable at all — and puts the cost of the wrong assumption into a complexity class rather than a seed spread. Mechanical rule for us: **before we promise that a white-box reading of our network is exact or minimal, we name the structural assumption that licenses it (additivity, bounded scope, sparse interaction graph), state whether our model satisfies it, and say what the guarantee degrades to when it does not — because "we can compute the minimal explanation" is a theorem about the architecture, and the architecture is the thing we chose.**
- **Pattern:** **The measure to steal is "solved inside budget", reported as a numerator and denominator next to every mean — because two of the paper's own tables show that a mean computed only over converged instances is a statement about which instances converged.** At interaction order 4 the mean size and mean time are computed on the 1,096 of 3,450 examples (31.8%) that finished inside an hour, and the authors flag the subset as probably biased toward easier inputs; under an unrestricted admissible domain the sensitivity-guided baseline drops to 13 of 61 solved while its reported mean size stays competitive with 51 of 61, so the mean would read as a fair loss and the denominator as a rout. The exact-baseline appendix applies the same discipline in the other direction: brute force and minimum-hitting-set are reported only where they are affordable (8 features), which is what makes the claim "close to the true optimum where an optimum exists" a checkable statement rather than an assertion. Read against run 739 (a Spearman that dropped to 0.70 on the ViT, where the aggregate hid a regime change) and run 743 (a record of how many cells beat a control without the magnitude): the recurrence is that **the aggregate is always honest about the instances that entered it and never about the ones that did not.** Mechanical rule for us: **every aggregate number we publish carries `n_solved/n_total` in the same cell, and where instances were dropped for timeout or convergence we state the drop rate and say which direction the surviving subset is biased — because "mean error over the runs that completed" and "mean error over the runs we attempted" are different measurements, and only the denominator distinguishes them.**
- **Pattern:** **Tightening the definition to buy tractability is legitimate here only because the paper refuses to sell the tightened thing as the thing — strong sufficiency is a strictly stronger property, so every strongly-sufficient explanation is genuinely sufficient, but it carries no size guarantee against a plain subset-minimal baseline, and the size table shows that: the method wins on a minority of the strong-sufficiency benchmarks and loses on Breast Cancer and California Housing.** The paper states this in the same paragraph that introduces the relaxation, and then introduces a *separate* setting (sparsity) where the guarantee is for the original model and where it wins on all nine. Read against run 744 (a rank-capped surrogate whose accuracy cost against the uncapped version is the price the headline omits) and run 742 (a decomposition made exact by construction so the sum and the joint effect coincide): those entries are about paying for exactness, this one is about **buying tractability with a stronger predicate and then keeping the two settings in separate tables.** Mechanical rule for us: **when we substitute a stricter, cheaper-to-verify predicate for the quantity we actually claim to measure, we print (a) the implication direction that makes it sound, (b) a head-to-head against a baseline on the original quantity, and (c) the cases where the substitute loses — because a conservative approximation can be certified and still be worse, and the certification is what makes it tempting to report only that.**

Two phrasings on the arXiv API returned nothing in CS: `abs:"interaction" AND abs:"order" AND abs:"identifiable"` (25 hits, all physics/chemistry/network-science, plus already-read `2611.26101`), `abs:"interaction order" AND abs:"neural network" AND abs:"identifiable"` (1 hit, a quantum tabular-advantage paper). - **Done:** arxiv-2606.08390 - When Are Neural Interaction Discoveries Real? Identifiability, Recoverability, and a Pre-Fit Diagnostic (read full v1 HTML: 55,621 chars / 406 lines plain-stripped, plus the raw 304,950-char HTML re-read and its 436 `<math alttext>` annotations substituted back in with `/tmp/lit/math8390.py` into a 464-line / 63,575-char text file, because this build strips every formula and every table cell exactly as in run 744; read: abstract, §I Introduction with all four contributions, §II Definitions 1-2, §III Lemmas 3-4 and Assumptions 5-6, §IV Proposition 7 with proof, §V-A Assumption 8, Proposition 9 with proof and Remark 10, §V-B Assumptions 11-12 and Lemma 13 with proof, §V-C Assumptions 14/17/20, Propositions 15/18/21 with proofs, Definitions 16/19 and Remark 22, §V-D Lemma 23, Proposition 24 and Theorem 25 (all three claims) with proofs, Corollary 26 with proof, §VI on the participation-ratio effective rank including the explicit rejection of condition number and thresholded rank, §VI-A workflow and Figure 1, §VI-B failure modes F1-F4, §VII setup / recovery at scale / additive baseline / capacity-matched comparison with Table I read cell by cell, the rho-sweep and Figure 3, §VIII-A-VIII-D with Tables II, III and IV read cell by cell, §IX Related Work, §X Discussion, §XI Conclusion, and the references for every work cited; Figures 1-4 read from captions and reporting prose, **not inspected as images**). **Note:** §VIII-A's *Setup B* paragraph and the Figure 4 caption are **truncated in the HTML build itself** (they break mid-sentence at "r_eff" and "Pairwise"), so the PM2.5-to-PM10 numbers are described as the paper's 6-of-8 win count only, and nothing is quoted from the missing tail.
- **Pattern:** **This is the first entry in the folder to give an *input-side* certificate of non-identifiability, and the certificate is cheap: participation-ratio effective rank of the joint lag-block covariance, computed from one eigendecomposition of the inputs before any training, predicts whether an interaction decomposition can be recovered at all.** The correlation sweep makes it a curve rather than a sign - effective rank falls from 3.00 to 1.54 as the two true modulators become collinear and modulator-set recovery collapses from noisy mid-range values to 0/10 seeds at the top, and the paper plots gate error against rank rather than against the correlation knob it swept, because rank is the quantity the theorem names. Read against run 745 (non-identifiability from non-rectangular support, named as a limitation after the fact) and run 744 (cost as the barrier to an exact decomposition), the distinction is that those entries discover non-identifiability *after* fitting or price it *in compute*, while this one predicts it from the inputs alone and locates the failure in the data rather than in the optimiser. Mechanical rule for us: **before we report any decomposition of the network's internals, we compute a pre-fit spread statistic on the inputs or activations it will be fitted on - effective rank or participation ratio over the relevant covariance - and print it next to the result, because a decomposition measured on collapsed support is a statement about our parameterisation, and the rank says so before we have spent the compute to find out.**
- **Pattern:** **The sharpest finding here inverts the folder's reading of magnitudes: under collapsed support the within-fit margins were *large* (1.9x to 5.8x) while the models disagreed with each other across seeds, and the paper calls this "confidently arbitrary" and argues it is a *stronger* signature of non-identifiability than small margins would be.** Its four pre-registered predictions for the volatility panel were stated before the fit; three held, and the one that failed - the prediction of small margins - failed in the dimension the theory does not require, which the authors say outright rather than reframing. On the development panel the failure is quieter and more instructive: effective rank is 4.47 of a possible 4, so the pre-fit screen says feasible, but the two seeds disagree on the top modulator, the gate slopes flip sign, and the model does not beat the additive baseline (1.26 vs 1.19) - the property that the screen cannot see by construction. Read against run 736 (a norm gap manufactured by a term in the loss) and run 737 (a coefficient sweep whose optimum the prose denied): those entries show a statistic being *produced* by a design choice, this one shows a statistic being *unreproducible* while looking strong. Mechanical rule for us: **a large effect size from a single fit is not evidence of anything until a second independent fit reproduces it, and we make the two-run comparison part of the report rather than a robustness footnote - because "confidently arbitrary" is the failure mode that survives every within-run check and dies only at the second seed.**
- **Pattern:** **The paper pushes the identifiability question up one level, from the components to the *order* of the interaction, and states the synthesis that makes it crisp: a three-way product restricted to a support where one input equals another is exactly a two-way function, so the order cannot be recovered from that data.** On full-dimensional product support a genuinely k-way function cannot be written as a sum of lower-order terms and the order is identified; under rank-collapsed support it can, and between the extremes strong correlation degrades order identifiability well before complete collapse. The paper also makes its own headroom explicit in a way that matters for us: a hierarchical functional-ANOVA decomposition gives "the order of a dependence" a representation-independent definition, but only under extra regularity assumptions that full-dimensional support does not imply - and the gated model's listed modulators need a separate faithfulness assumption to bridge the structure to the parameters. Read against run 740 (26% of the target preserved, so the experiment was a boundary case) and run 742 (a partition exact by construction, so sum-of-parts and joint effect coincide): the recurrence is that **every "this is what the component means" claim rests on a bridge assumption that the headline theorem does not carry.** Mechanical rule for us: **whenever we claim an interaction of a particular order, we state the support condition that makes the order identifiable and the bridge assumption that makes the fitted parameters mean that structure, and we say which of the two is doing the work - because "the decomposition is unique" and "the fitted parameters are that decomposition" are separate theorems and the first is routinely reported as if it were the second.**

- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's cleanest demonstration that a net signed number has an *arbitrary zero*, and it arrives with a construction rather than an argument: on a synthetic function whose interesting coefficient sits behind a third-order interaction with zero expectation, the population one-dimensional partial dependence for that feature is identically zero, so every model's standard chart is flat while the effect is strong.** Only the paper's magnitude summary - the shared positive backbone, which cannot cancel by construction - retains the quadratic shape. Worse for the signed reading, in the unconstrained rank-1 alternative each individual factor is only identifiable up to a joint sign flip, so a fit exists in which the features' own curves look negative while the product is positive because four signs cancel. Read against run 742 (the signed gross/net budget, where a net of 0.05 could be a mechanism or an absence and only the pair distinguished them) and run 741 (attribution as a pair of estimands), the distinction is that those entries say a net hides its components, while this one shows a marginal operation erases a component outright and shows a quantity that survives. Mechanical rule for us: **every component score we publish carries a magnitude summary that is non-negative by construction alongside the signed one, the two are reported together, and we state which gauge fixed the sign - because a signed number whose zero point is set by a gauge choice is not a measurement, and a signed net whose components cancel to zero reads identically to a component that is absent.**
- **Pattern:** **The paper's principal stated limitation is that its own model is not identifiable, and it lists three distinct mechanisms - scaling and permutation, non-rectangular support, and noisy fitting - then shows that positivity fixes only the first two partially and that the third causes whole bags to converge to different factorisations of the same function.** The consequence is mechanical: some fitted bags are discarded rather than averaged, so only a fraction of the ensemble contributes, across-seed variance is high, and the recommended fix (aligning all bags on the positive rank-1 manifold) is left as future work. The paper states plainly that the latent factors should not be read as uniquely recoverable objects but as one admissible representation chosen by the fitting procedure. Read against run 744 (a decomposition whose *cost* is exponential in order) and run 743 (a remainder that is only ever measured, never bounded), the recurrence is that **every exact-looking decomposition in this folder arrives with a caveat about which of its parts are real** - and this is the first entry to catalogue the caveats as separate mechanisms with different fixes. Mechanical rule for us: **before we report any decomposed component as a fact about the model, we run the decomposition at no fewer than two seeds or two initialisations, align them in a named gauge, and report the between-run disagreement in the component values themselves - because two runs that fit equally well and disagree on a factor have produced two admissible representations, and reporting either of them alone as the structure is a statement about our optimiser, not about the network.** Cross-reference run 740 (a decomposition that preserved only 26% of the target and was labelled a boundary case).
- **Pattern:** **The cost boundary carried out of run 744 is crossed here, but in the direction the folder had not considered: the escape from exponential subset enumeration is not a cheaper exact decomposition, it is a *different function class* with a hard rank cap - and the paper's own benchmark shows the cap costing accuracy.** Training cost is stated as linear in stages, bagged grids, split iterations, samples and features, with the bagged grids embarrassingly parallel and histogram binning cutting the per-iteration sample dependence; but every stage contributes at most two products, so the model's separation rank is bounded at twice the stage count, and across 27 datasets the family's unconstrained version wins more aggregate rows than the positivity-constrained one the paper advocates. The approximation rate carries its own caveat: it is dimension-free only for a target class whose required order of mixed differentiability grows with the dimension. Read against run 742 (completeness by construction, where the sum of parts *is* the joint effect) and run 744 (exponential subset cost, left open), the distinction is that those entries ask whether a decomposition is exact and how much it costs, this one shows a cheap decomposition is available only by restricting the hypothesis class. Mechanical rule for us: **when we trade an exponential exact decomposition for a tractable one, we publish the rank or order cap that makes it tractable, the accuracy lost against the uncapped version at matched budget, and a sentence naming which function class the guarantee is for - because "scalable decomposition" and "exact decomposition" are answers to different questions, and the cap is the price that is never in the headline.**

- **Pattern:** **This is the first entry in the folder where the "interaction term" of run 743 is computed EXACTLY and then used as a *developmental* statistic - and the developmental claim is the sharpest thing in it: the normalised cross-layer component is flat at its baseline (ratio ~1) until roughly 2.1 billion tokens of pretraining, and only then separates upward with bootstrap bands that no longer contain 1.** Across all seven models the ratio is above 1 at every order (weakest Pythia-1B order 2 at 1.62, strongest Pythia-1.4B 60.8 and Pythia-6.9B 64.4 at order 6), and the baseline itself halves per order (0.30 / 0.11 / 0.05 / 0.03 / 0.02 in four of the seven families) while the final checkpoint stops decaying. Read against run 743 (a cross-layer remainder measured pointwise on a frozen checkpoint and shown to be as large as the same-block sum) and run 739 (a spectral statistic whose onset leads behavioural degradation), the distinction is that 743 measures *how big* the cross-layer term is on one trained model while this one measures *when it appears* across a training trajectory. Mechanical rule for us: **any structural quantity our white-box model reports is measured at no fewer than three checkpoints of the same run and reported as a ratio to the earliest checkpoint, with the episode at which the ratio stops straddling 1 named - because a quantity that is already present at initialisation is a property of the architecture, and only the trajectory separates architecture from learned computation.** Cross-reference run 738 (head geometry vs feature geometry under one knob).
- **Pattern:** **The control here is the folder's best-designed intervention control and should become our default: a norm-matched random direction deleted at the same site, so the comparison isolates direction and nothing else.** Component deletion beats the random control in all 35 cells (Pythia-1.4B order 6: 224x vs 19.8x; Pythia-6.9B order 5: 1472x vs 162x; OLMo-1B order 2: 218x vs 18.6x), but Amber-7B's margins are 1.1x vs 1.04x at order 4 and 1.8x vs 1.20x at order 6, and Pythia-6.9B order 6 is 1253x vs 898x - a factor of 1.4 - in the same table where the effect is three orders of magnitude elsewhere. The paper reports the cell count as the result and does not comment on the size spread. Read against run 736 (a seen/unseen split that carried the whole claim) and run 733 (a headline 0.0117 against a 0.0085 seed spread): the recurrence is that **a sign-consistent table can hide an effect of any magnitude, and the count of consistent cells is not the effect size.** Mechanical rule for us: **every intervention experiment we run pairs a deletion with a norm-matched random direction at the same site, and we publish the ratio-of-ratios (component damage divided by control damage) next to each cell rather than only the count of cells where the sign holds - because "beats the control in 35 of 35" and "beats the control by 11x median and 1.4x in the worst cell" are different claims and the second is the one a reader can act on.**
- **Pattern:** **Representation similarity is negatively associated with joint computation in all seven models, yet it is not a substitute for it, and the paper's block-removal table shows the cheap proxy pointing at the wrong block in the strongest model.** Pairwise CKA correlates negatively with the pairwise connected component everywhere (Pythia-410M about -0.52 to -0.71 by order; Pythia-6.9B only -0.19 at order 2), and the CKA-based block ranking correlation vanishes or flips on Amber-7B (+0.18, +0.01) while the coordination-based one stays positive (+0.57, +0.49); concretely, CKA's "similar, therefore redundant" pick on Amber order 2 costs 55.7 perplexity against 26.9 for its "dissimilar" pick, while the low-coordination pick costs 23.8. In the Pythia family the two criteria agrees within 8% in 10 of 15 cells, so the failure is model-specific rather than general. Read against run 734 (eight collapse scalars used as covariates to pick a detector family - a proxy used as a selector) and run 728 (a near/mid/far stratification where the winner changes by regime): the recurrence is that **a proxy validated as a correlate is then used as a decision rule, and it is the decision rule that breaks.** Mechanical rule for us: **when we use a cheap structural proxy (similarity, norm, rank) to decide what to remove, patch or prune, we validate it by actually performing the removal and scoring the outcome, per model, and we report the cells where the proxy and the direct measure disagree - because a correlation with the true quantity does not license the ranking, and the ranking is what the proxy is for.**

The HTML build rendered the maths inline this time, so Eq. 4-6 and the Table 1 cells were readable without a PDF fallback.
- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's first exact, complete accounting of a *multi-layer* interaction, and its verdict overturns the assumption the folder has been operating on since run 742: the same-block sum is not a proxy for the joint effect once components sit in different layers.** Pooled over 1,440 measurements the median same-block sum (0.31 logits) sits right next to the median total interaction (0.30), which reads as confirmation - but the median cross-layer remainder is 0.59, roughly twice either, and the same-block sum exceeds the total on 57% of pairs while the remainder exceeds it on 63%, so the two routinely cancel; one configuration shows a same-block sum of 3.85 logits against a total of 0.11. Read against run 742 (a decomposition made complete by construction, so sum-of-parts and joint effect coincide by theorem) and run 741 (the cross-interaction invisible to per-head measurement, group term dwarfing the pairwise), the distinction is the sharpest in the arc: those entries establish that *within* a partition the total is the sum, this one establishes that across layers the naive partition misses a term of the same order or larger. Mechanical rule for us: **every multi-component ablation we publish prints three columns - the sum of per-component effects, the measured joint effect, and the residual as its own number with its own magnitude - and we never report the sum as a prediction of the joint effect unless the residual has been measured to be small on our own model rather than assumed small by analogy to a single-block theorem.**
- **Pattern:** **Two rigorous, pointwise-verified, never-violated bounds are looser than the truth by 71x and by 10^5-10^6x, and the paper reports the ratio rather than the pass - which makes this the folder's cleanest case that a bound is a certificate of order, not an estimate.** The attention Jacobian bound's p-weighted form exceeds the finite-difference estimate by 12x to 111x (median 71x) on Qwen's real weights over 12 sites and 24 random unit perturbations each, with 0 violations; its weight-only normalization-closed form is finite and also never violated but looser by four to six orders of magnitude (median 1.7e5, max 4.2e6), and is described as "not a numerically useful estimate as stated". The curvature bound has 0 violations in 1,200 samples but exceeds the largest sampled curvature by 6.5e2 to 2.4e3, and the paper localises the slack: the normalization lemma alone is loose by only 12.9-15.7x, so the slack accumulates in the product-of-norms composition, not in the derivative. Read against run 731 (a per-sample bound repaired so unsupported inputs stay in the ranking) and run 725 (a bound whose failing constant was estimated rather than argued away): the recurrence is that **a bound's job and an estimate's job are different, and only one of them can carry a quantitative claim.** Mechanical rule for us: **every closed-form bound our white-box model asserts is published with (bound value, measured value at the observation point, ratio) side by side, and the ratio - not the absence of violations - decides whether it may be used to predict; where the ratio is above two orders of magnitude the bound is labelled a certificate of order and quoted no further.**
- **Pattern:** **The paper names a negative result with a structural reason rather than an empirical failure: the cross-layer remainder is a bilinear form, so no function of the two marginal magnitudes can predict its sign, and it records this as a closed question rather than an unexplored one.** Differentiating the interpolation twice at the origin shows the mixed derivative is a pairing between how much of the earlier layer's removed mass reaches the later layer's input and how sensitive the later layer's removed-mass map is there; neither the product nor the sum of the two single-site effects is such a pairing, so neither should be expected to give the sign "as a rule". The check is then explicit about its own worthlessness - the sign matches on two of three instances and fails on the third, and the paper says three points settle nothing and the check was free. Read against run 740 (a decomposition that preserved 26% of the target and was reported as a boundary case) and run 738 (an ablation that confirmed a mechanism and then showed the mechanism was not load-bearing): those entries report a measurement that failed to support a claim; this one reports a claim that was *shown unpromising before* the measurement and then measured anyway. Mechanical rule for us: **when a structural argument tells us a simple relation cannot hold, we write it into the notes as a negative result with the reason, and we do not spend a run searching for the relation empirically - and when we do run the check anyway because it is free, we print the sample size next to the result so a 2-of-3 match cannot be read as a pattern.**

Note the sign: the paper's "cancellation factor" is a **gross-to-net ratio with 1 meaning no cancellation**, not a fraction cancelled - worth remembering before quoting it.
- **Pattern:** **This is the answer to the compositionality carry, and the answer is an arithmetic guarantee rather than a learned correction: a contribution defined on full input-to-output path terms sums exactly to the output change at every granularity, so "sum of the parts" and "joint effect" are the same quantity by construction rather than by fit.** Hierarchical completeness holds for any family of subnetworks whose path sets partition a larger one, which makes neurons, edges, convolutional channels and arbitrary circuits all readable as marginals of a single path-level matrix, and recovers Integrated Gradients, neuron conductance and CODEC as special cases of one definition. Read against run 741 (which proves the cross-interaction is invisible to per-head measurement and that the group term dwarfs any pairwise term) and run 740 (a decomposition that preserved only 26% of the target signal), the distinction is that those two entries measure how badly a decomposition approximates a behavioural quantity while this one builds the decomposition so that it cannot. Mechanical rule for us: **we do not fit a correction to close the gap between a sum of component scores and the measured group effect; we define our scores on units whose partition is exact, verify the partition sums to the measured output difference to within the printed numerical error, and report that error - because a decomposition that is complete by construction makes the sum-of-parts-versus-joint question disappear, and one that is incomplete by 74% cannot be repaired by any downstream model.** Cross-reference run 733 (a theorem predicting an ablation it then measured).
- **Pattern:** **The signed gross/net budget makes cancellation measurable and shows it is the norm, not the exception - so a small net contribution is not evidence of a weak component, and the folder has been reading small numbers as small effects.** At the path level, gross excitatory and inhibitory sums are much larger than the residual that neuron-level methods report, the cancellation factor roughly triples from edges to paths, and the distribution coincides across three random seeds; the "silent" neuron that motivated the analysis turns out to be strongly driven in both directions on exactly the inputs where it looks inert. In the retina the gross drive exceeds net output by a median factor of 6.2 and inhibition supplies 45% of it, with the strongest inhibitory paths converging on the same channels as the strongest excitatory ones. Read against run 741 (two estimands whose difference is itself the measurement) and run 736 (a norm gap manufactured by a loss term): the recurrence is that **a quantity defined as a difference silently discards one of its two terms, and the discarded term is often as large as the retained one.** Mechanical rule for us: **every intervention result we publish carries (gross positive, gross negative, net) at the finest granularity we can afford, and a component whose net is near zero is described as cancelling rather than as unimportant until the gross budget says otherwise - because a net of 0.05 from +3.0 and -2.95 is a mechanism, and the same 0.05 from +0.05 and 0.00 is an absence, and only the pair distinguishes them.** Cross-reference run 739 (a ratio that saturates while its numerator grows threefold - normalisation deciding what a number means).
- **Pattern:** **Bulk patching lands on the diagonal against summed contributions while single-context patching does not sum correctly, and that asymmetry is the cleanest statement in the folder of why group interventions need group scores.** Averaged over 3,001 simulated contexts the path contribution tracks the patching effect closely, and the difference between them is much smaller than the spread across contexts, so the contribution behaves as an efficient summary of a distribution that is expensive to sample; but remove-one and keep-one patching each fail to sum to the output (the XOR toy model counts the product term twice under one convention and never under the other), and only complete contributions recover it. The paper also shows the search must be exact rather than heuristic: a beam search over the most important edges has to enumerate about 100 times more paths than requested to find the true top ones, and 100 times more again over neurons. Read against run 734 (a random shortlist of matched size matching a fitted predictor) and run 728 (a stratification where the winner changes by regime): those entries establish that selectors are only as good as their shortlist, this one establishes that a best-first search with a provable bound can be cheaper than the heuristic it replaces while returning the exact answer. Mechanical rule for us: **any group intervention we report is paired with the summed per-component score for the same group, the comparison is printed as a scatter against the diagonal rather than as a correlation, and where a cheap heuristic stands in for an exact search we report how much extra enumeration it costs to reach the exact answer - because a diagonal scatter says the score predicts the intervention and a correlation only says the two move together.**

- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's sharpest statement that an attribution is a *pair* of estimands, not a number, and that the gap between them is the measurement - arriving with a closed-form reason rather than a correlation.** Denoising and noising are not two recipes for one quantity: the paper proves the first estimates the pure indirect effect (bypass edges blocked) while the second estimates that effect *plus* a mediated interaction equal to the mixed second directional derivative of the downstream map in the two perturbation directions. So the gap is not estimator error to be reduced; it is a property of the model at that operating point. Read against run 740 (probe readout moved 0.24 while behaviour moved on ~6% of items) and run 739 (an exactly-scored influence whose Spearman dropped from 0.96 to 0.70 on the ViT), the recurrence is now three deep and the shape is identical: **two instruments aimed at the "same" causal quantity disagree, and the disagreement is the finding.** Mechanical rule for us: **every causal attribution we publish is reported as an ordered pair from both intervention directions on the same component, with the difference reported as its own quantity and the perturbation distance named - because a single-direction attribution silently adds a term whose size we do not know, and a term that is invisible at small patch distance becomes dominant at larger distances without any change to the model.** Cross-reference run 733 (an ablation that moved the measured geometry without moving AUROC in the predicted direction).
- **Pattern:** **The paper converts a nuisance into a free functional label, and that conversion is directly reusable: the *sign* of the interaction term sorts heads into three mechanistically distinct roles that neither estimator recovers alone.** Negative interaction with positive pure effect is backup compensation (the head's marginal contribution shrinks because a peer is doing the same job); positive interaction with near-zero pure effect is a context-specific mechanism whose whole contribution is realised only when the downstream circuit is intact; negative pure effect with positive interaction is a harmful mechanism partially opposed by its peers. The signs are consistent within functional groups, so clustering on the pair recovers the published labels without re-reading the circuit paper. Read against run 734 (eight collapse scalars used as covariates to pick a detector family) and run 737 (a coefficient swept to move collapse and behaviour together): those entries use a scalar as a *predictor*; this one uses a *signed difference* as a *classifier*, and the classifier's categories are causal roles rather than scores. Mechanical rule for us: **when a white-box statistic has two natural reference points (two directions, two baselines, two ablations), we compute the signed contrast and report it as a categorical variable with its role interpretation attached, rather than collapsing it into a magnitude - because the magnitude answers "how much" and the sign answers "what kind", and downstream analysis almost always needs the second.**
- **Pattern:** **The measured quantity depends on the experimental design choices the researcher makes, and three of the four sufficient conditions for it to vanish are choices - which makes "clean single-component attribution" a statement about the experiment, not the model.** The interaction term is provably zero when the component is unaffected by the input change, when the downstream map is locally affine, or when the two perturbations have no shared component in the curved directions; and empirically it is small exactly for semantically close prompt pairs, for single-word counterfactuals, and for piecewise-affine compiled models. The paper states the consequence plainly: the magnitude of the phenomenon is set by the evaluation conditions rather than by the circuit, and benchmark suites built on compiled transformers cannot exhibit it at all. Read against run 739 (a threshold grid was the only thing that showed a claimed timing lead survives its own free parameters) and run 737 (a coefficient swept to locate an interior optimum its own prose denied): the recurrence is that **a free parameter in the experimental design silently determines the sign of the result, and the paper that names it is the exception.** Mechanical rule for us: **any conclusion of the form "component X is responsible for behaviour Y" is printed alongside the corruption scheme, the prompt distance and the ablation baseline that produced it, and where more than one of those was varied we report the conclusion per setting - because the same component ranks first under one corruption scheme and one hundred and forty-second under another, and neither number is wrong.**

- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's sharpest case that the instrument and the behaviour are two different measurements of the same intervention, and the dissociation is the finding: probe-only features moved the probe's own readout by 0.24 while moving behaviour on about 6 percent of statements, and shared features did the reverse (0.05 readout shift, 13-27 percent behaviour).** Read against run 736 (a norm gap manufactured by a mask in the loss), run 734 (eight geometric scalars predicting which detector family wins) and run 739 (a spectral statistic whose onset leads behaviour), the difference is that those entries measure a *property* of a state and this one measures two *outcomes* of one ablation. Mechanical rule for us: **every intervention we report is scored twice - on the model's output and on the statistic we are validating - and where the two disagree we report the pair as the result rather than picking the favourable one, because a feature set that moves the probe and not the output is proof the probe is a readout, and that is information we would otherwise discard.** Cross-reference run 733 (a compaction ablation that moved the measured geometry without moving AUROC in the predicted direction) - the same table shape, drawn out explicitly.
- **Pattern:** **The cheapest attribution signal in the folder - gradient sensitivity - earns nothing here, and the reason it earns nothing is stated: a gradient is a first-order local quantity, so it can name features that carry the wrong sign, that never fire, or that sit off the manifold the model actually visits.** Gradient-top features flip behaviour on about 6 percent, statistically indistinguishable from the activation-free geometric top-16's 6.1 percent, while the *intersection* of the two rankings flips 13-27 percent and an activation-aware selection flips 17.6. Read against run 731 (a bound repaired so unsupported inputs stay in the ranking) and run 739 (an exactly-scored per-example influence with a stated approximation error), the recurrence is that a first-order object is used as if it were the effect: one entry repairs the first-order account, this entry shows it cannot stand alone. Mechanical rule for us: **a feature-importance or attribution score is never reported as a predictor until it has been paired with a second, structurally different signal (an activation statistic, an ablation, or a downstream readout) and the pair is scored against the single signals - because a first-order score that ranks identically to a static geometric one is not measuring sensitivity, it is measuring which direction is largest.** Cross-reference run 728 (a near/mid/far stratification where the winner changes by regime).
- **Pattern:** **The paper's auxiliary experiment fails its own precondition and is reported as a boundary case rather than a null result - the folder's best example of gating a causal claim behind a decomposition check.** Only about 26 percent of the Qwen3-8B reward-hacking probe's margin survived the SAE reconstruction, with 74 percent in the reconstruction error, so ablating "probe-aligned" SAE features flipped roughly a third of predictions *in reconstruction space* and almost none on raw activations. The lesson is mechanical: an SAE-based intervention is a statement about the SAE's basis, and it says nothing about the model until you check that the quantity you are trying to explain lives inside that basis. Read against run 738 (a mechanism ablation that barely moved behaviour, reported honestly) and run 730 (a negative result repaired by measuring whether the phenomenon was expressible): three different ways of asking the same question - *was the thing we tried to manipulate even there?* Mechanical rule for us: **before any decomposition-based causal claim we print the share of the target quantity that the decomposition preserves, and if that share is small the experiment is labelled a boundary case with the number attached rather than reported as evidence either way - because a 26-percent-preserved signal guarantees a null on raw activations and a positive result on the reconstruction, and only the printed fraction tells a reader which of those two tables they are looking at.** Cross-reference run 719-style "was the effect expressible" checks generally.

- **Done:** arxiv-2607.12438 - Fisher Rank Inflation: A Spectral Signature of Memorization under Label Noise (read full v1 HTML, 157,534 chars / 2,207 lines: abstract, §1-§6 including the complete §5 Discussion with all four limitations and §6 Conclusion, §2 Related Work, §3.1-§3.9 with Lemma 1-5, Theorem 1 (both cases), Theorem 2's condition, Theorem 3, Proposition 1 and the majorization/NIR distinction, §4.1 setup with the subsampling protocol and metric definitions, §4.2-§4.7 including Table 1 (clean-difficulty control), Table 2 (cross-architecture), Table 3 (direct spectral diagnostics) and Table 4 (CIFAR-10N), Appendix A.6 with Tables 9, A.9 with Table 10 and the nine-cell onset-threshold grid, A.10 with Table 11 (centred vs uncentred Fisher); the deferred proofs and Appendix sections A.1-A.5, A.7-A.8 were **not** read beyond the statements they are cited from; Figures 1-12 read from captions and reporting prose, **not inspected as images**; reference list not read beyond works cited in §1-§2)
- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's first entry where a purely weight- and gradient-side statistic visibly moves while the behavioural one on the same network has not yet moved, and it arrives with a timing claim rather than a correlation - which is the shape a predictive white-box model needs.** Rank inflation onset precedes test degradation under every one of nine onset-threshold pairs for both CIFAR-10 convolutions and CIFAR-100 ResNet18 (mean lead 22.0 to 35.7 epochs, positive fraction 1.00), and only becomes threshold-dependent for the ViT (6/9, range including negative values). The authors deliberately downgrade this to a retrospective comparison and refuse to sell it as an online predictor, which is the discipline. Read against run 737 (a coefficient swept with an error next to it, but no timing) and run 738 (shallow and deep forgetting measures plotted against one knob), the difference is that those two measure two statistics of the *same* moment while this one measures two statistics at *different* moments and reports the lag. Mechanical rule for us: **when our white-box model produces a statistic that is meant to lead behaviour, we report the onset of the statistic and the onset of the behaviour as two separately-defined epochs with the thresholds named, and we state the fraction of threshold settings on which the lead is positive - because a single default threshold pair will produce a lead time whether or not one exists, and the threshold grid is the only thing that shows whether the claim survives its own free parameters.** Cross-reference run 730 (a null repaired by measuring whether the phenomenon was expressible).
- **Pattern:** **The leave-one-out-with-re-centring closure makes an exactly scored per-example influence on any spectral statistic of a scatter matrix cheap, and that is the single most reusable technical object in this entry for us.** Deleting one example from a centred scatter has the closed form "remove a rank-one term and rescale", so `er(S) - er(S_{-i})` needs no retraining; the derivative of effective rank with respect to the eigenvalues is written out as a signed sensitivity that is positive exactly on eigendirections carrying less than the reciprocal of the effective rank in normalised mass, and the per-example score is then the centred gradient's quadratic form against that sensitivity matrix. The paper then validates the cheap score against the exact recomputation: Spearman 0.9795 (SmallCNN) and 0.9603 (ResNet18), degrading to 0.7036 (ViT) and 0.6514 (CIFAR-100), and says plainly that higher-order deletion effects explain the degradation. Read against run 728 (a near/mid/far stratification where the winner changes by regime) and run 734 (eight scalars used as covariates predicting a detector clique): those entries use checkpoint scalars only in the aggregate, whereas this one converts a scalar into a per-example quantity with a stated approximation error, which is the granularity our paper's "prediction under intervention" claim actually needs. Mechanical rule for us: **any white-box statistic we report at the model level is accompanied by its per-input or per-example decomposition and by the correlation between that cheap decomposition and the exact recomputed version, with the regimes where the correlation drops called out as the regimes where the first-order account fails - because a statistic that ranks examples well in one architecture family and near chance in another (0.96 vs 0.70 here) has told us the approximation, not the phenomenon, is architecture-dependent.** Cross-reference run 736 (a closed-form gradient account of where a geometry comes from) - here the closed form is for the *influence*, not for the geometry.
- **Pattern:** **The same sweep produces one monotone statistic and one saturating-then-falling statistic, and the difference is trace normalisation - which makes this the folder's cleanest demonstration that how you normalise a spectral quantity decides whether it carries a trend at all.** Peak effective rank rises monotonically over the corruption sweep, 28.88 -> 62.69 -> 70.37 -> 78.98 -> 87.90 -> 97.09, while the noisy-to-clean effective-rank ratio sits near 2.00 across the low end and *declines* to 1.87 at the highest corruption, which the paper reports in a single sentence and does not explain. The paper is careful elsewhere about exactly this distinction - §3.7 spends a full subsection arguing that the ratio (computed after centring each group separately) is logically distinct from the globally centred additive decomposition, and Proposition 1 gives only a one-sided majorisation guarantee `NIR >= 1` that cannot license a monotone claim. Read against run 735 (per-class vs per-sample loss normalisation as the load-bearing variable, where the same architecture with a different objective produced a completely different similarity matrix) and run 731 (a deficit average hiding the losing cells): the recurrence is now three entries deep and the lesson has generalised from losses to statistics - **a normalisation is a modelling choice with a directional consequence, and it is almost never the one the paper's headline is about.** Mechanical rule for us: **every spectral or geometric statistic we publish is printed in raw and trace-normalised form side by side, any monotonicity claim names which of the two it holds for, and where the two disagree we treat the raw one as the sweep's signal and the normalised one as a measure of shape - because trace normalisation divides out exactly the magnitude that grows with the knob, and a ratio that saturates near 2 while its numerator grows threefold is measuring shape, not amount.** Cross-reference run 737 (a coefficient sweep whose optimum was interior and whose flatness was misread as insensitivity).

- **Done:** arxiv-2512.07400 - Heads collapse, features stay: Why Replay needs big buffers (read full v2 HTML, 137,213 chars / 2,403 lines: abstract, §1-§6 including Limitations and Broader Implications, §1.1, §2, §3.1-§3.2 with the multi-head subsection, §4.1-§4.3 with Hypotheses 1-2, Definition 1, Theorems 1-3, Corollaries 1-2, the under-determined-classifier argument and §4.3's LDA decomposition, §5, Appendix A.1 with Tables 1-3, A.2, A.3.1-A.3.3 with Table 4 and the norm-matching ablation, A.4 captions; Appendices B-C read only as headings; Figures 1-24 read from captions and reporting prose, **not inspected as images**; reference list read only as far as works cited)
- **Pattern:** **This is the folder's cleanest single-knob experiment on a two-part decomposition, and it delivers the shape our white-box model needs: one control variable, two statistics of the same network, and a theory that says which of them moves first.** Buffer size is swept over ten settings; deep forgetting (a logistic probe on frozen features) saturates at the smallest non-zero buffer while shallow forgetting (the network's own accuracy on past tasks) keeps degrading until near-full replay, so the gap between the two curves is the finding and the sweep is the whole table. Read against run 737 (a collapse coefficient swept on a ten-class appendix with an interior optimum) and run 734 (eight NC scalars used as covariates predicting a detector clique), the methodological advance is small but real: the knob here is *data availability*, not a loss coefficient, so the manipulation is a change in support rather than a change in the objective, and that makes it the closest experiment in the folder to our question of what a model does on inputs whose support it has lost. Mechanical rule for us: **when we claim a white-box statistic tracks behaviour, we report it alongside a matched statistic computed on the *other* half of the model's decomposition (features vs readout), both plotted against the same controlled knob, because a statistic that saturates at a tenth of the knob's range while the behaviour does not is a statistic about the representation, and calling it a predictor of the behaviour is the mistake this paper avoids by printing both curves.** Cross-reference run 736 (a training term that manufactures the geometry) and run 735 (head geometry generated by class support).
- **Pattern:** **The paper's own ablation confirms a mechanism and then reports that removing it barely changes the behaviour - the inverse of the folder's usual defect, and the more useful version, because it kills the theory's practical claim in one figure.** Appendix A.3.3 attributes the monotone growth of the centred class-mean norm to initialisation shock (new heads created with smaller norms than converged ones), shows that norm-matching initialisation suppresses the growth, and then states that final forgetting and test accuracy barely move. The theory says this norm sits in the denominator of the separability bound, so suppressing it should have helped; the paper prints the contradiction rather than burying it. Against run 737 (a mechanism ablation where every row moved something, in opposite directions) and run 736 (a term whose only stake was the sign of the effect), the difference is the kind of ablation: those perturbed the *objective*, this one perturbs an *artefact* of the training schedule and shows the artefact was not load-bearing. Mechanical rule for us: **we require every causal claim in our write-up to name the ablation that would falsify it, and when that ablation is run and brings a negligible change we record the claim as failing even though the mechanism is real - because "the mechanism exists" and "the mechanism is what moves the output" are separate claims, and only the second one supports a prediction.**

- **Done:** arxiv-2502.10691 - Controlling Neural Collapse Enhances Out-of-Distribution Detection and Transfer Learning (read full v3 HTML, 148,765 chars / 5,848 lines: abstract, §1-§8 including the complete §7 Discussion, §2.1-§2.3, §3 with the three error definitions and the energy score, §4.1 with Proposition 4.1 and the Kozachenko-Leonenko nearest-neighbour entropy estimate, §4.2 with the rank-(D-1) simplex ETF formula, §5 setup, §6.1-§6.6 with Tables 1-9 reconstructed from the column-major cells, Appendix A-E, Appendix G with Tables 13-14 including every per-dataset cell, Appendix H.1 Table 15; Figures 1-9 read from captions and reporting prose, **not inspected as images**; reference list not read beyond works cited in §1-§2)
- **Pattern:** **This is the causal test the folder has been carrying since run 735, and it arrives in a cheaper form than anyone expected: the collapse level of a layer is a training knob, and the cleanest controlled comparison needs only ONE training run because the encoder and the projector in the same network sit at opposite ends of that knob.** VGG17 trained with an entropy penalty on the encoder and a frozen simplex-ETF projector gives NC1 0.393 in the projector and 2.175 in the encoder, with detection error 65.10 against 87.62 and transfer error 66.36 against 41.85 — same weights, two embeddings, two behavioural errors, and the sign is exactly what the geometry predicts. Against runs 735 and 736, which established that geometry is an *output* of the objective, this entry supplies the opposite arrow: the objective is a dial, and moving the dial moves the behaviour. Mechanical rule for us: **when our white-box model reports a geometric statistic and attributes a behavioural effect to it, we first ask whether that statistic can be moved by a training term we control, and if it can we report the manipulation rather than the correlation — because a statistic that responds to a single coefficient is a knob, and a knob is a better claim than a slope.** Cross-reference run 736 (a loss term that manufactures the norm gap in closed form), run 734 (self-duality of the head carrying the detector prediction).
- **Pattern:** **The one place the axis is swept properly is buried in an appendix on a ten-class dataset, and it is the only evidence in the paper that the relation is a curve rather than a sign — which is the same defect as run 733's single compaction point and run 731's single bound value, now in its sixth appearance.** Table 14 on ImageNet-10 VGG17 with the entropy coefficient at 0 / 0.1 / 0.2 / 0.6 / 1.0: effective rank 2211.99 -> 4815.32, transfer error 77.78 -> 64.96 and ID error 9.20 -> 12.80. Most of the transfer gain is in the first 0.1 (77.78 -> 68.87), after which the curve is nearly flat, while the ID cost keeps rising roughly linearly — and transfer at 1.0 is *worse* than at 0.6 (64.96 against 65.15), so the optimum is interior and the paper's own sentence that the method is "not sensitive to coefficient" is a claim its own table contradicts at the top of the range. Note also that the main trade-off claim rests on one point per configuration with no seeds and no variance. Read against run 733 (an ablation inside the seed spread), run 729 (a correlation over surviving seeds) and run 731 (a deficit table whose summary hid the losing cells): **the folder now has six entries where the number that would settle the claim exists but is either unpublished, unswept, or averaged away.** Mechanical rule for us: **any claim that a mechanism improves a behaviour is accompanied by that behaviour plotted against the mechanism's own strength at no fewer than three settings, with the optimum located on the curve rather than assumed at the setting we chose — because a monotone-sounding method claim and an interior optimum are different claims, and a table that sweeps is the only thing that tells them apart.** Cross-reference run 732 (a tuned k whose optimum degenerated to 1).
- **Pattern:** **Every mechanism ablation moves two or three behaviours in different directions, and the paper prints all of them, which makes it the folder's clearest case that "improves X" needs X named per row — including one pair where the more collapsed model is also the more accurate one.** The frozen ETF projector beats a trainable projector by 8.9 detection points, loses 2.47 on transfer and *wins* 2.48 on ID error; group norm beats batch norm by 10.11 transfer and 4.37 detection while raising NC1 from 1.401 to 2.175; CE beats MSE by 6.74 detection and 17.71 transfer; and the encoder's weaker collapse costs 3.34 points of ID error on VGG17 and 0.06 on ViT-Tiny. So collapse is not a single axis for the model's behaviour — it is rewarded by detection, punished by transfer, and rewarded by in-distribution accuracy in at least one pairing. Read against run 728 (a manipulation that moved a phenomenon in opposite directions across two architectures, reported honestly) and run 734 (near/mid/far strata where the detector winner changes by regime): **the shape claim survives every sweep while the direction claim flips by task, architecture or pair.** Mechanical rule for us: **a geometry change we publish is reported against every behavioural metric in the study simultaneously — including the in-distribution one — and where two metrics move in opposite directions we print the pair rather than a summary, because a mechanism that helps detection, hurts transfer and helps accuracy is not "beneficial" or "harmful" and cannot be reported as either.** Cross-reference run 731 (an average that was best while a third of the cells were losses).

The thing three entries have named independently and nothing has attempted is **a probe whose score is invariant to the training term that produced the geometry** — either computed on held-out behaviour rather than on the embedding, or normalised by the very quantity the objective inflates. This entry makes the target sharper: the knob changes effective rank by more than 2x, so an invariance test has an obvious quantity to be invariant to.

- **Pattern:** **This is the first entry in the arc where the known-vs-unknown separation statistic is something the training objective MANUFACTURES, and the paper prints the accounting for it in closed form - which converts run 734's "geometry measures the loss" warning into an explicit mechanism.** Appendix A writes the norm derivative for every loss in the stack (cross-entropy, consistency, OVA) as a cosine against the relevant weight vector times the gradient magnitude, so norm growth is confidence-times-alignment; the non-alignment term multiplies the positive cosine term by zero and leaves only the tangent-plane projection, so uncertain and OOD items accumulate norm along a direction no prototype occupies. The visible consequence is a clean norm gap between known and unknown (their Figure 2b) plus a low-cosine/low-norm void (Figure 2c). Read against run 734 (collapsed-and-self-dual representation predicts which detector family suits a model), run 733 (a score hurt by over-compaction at a spread ratio of 7.5) and run 732 (a density score whose tuned k collapsed to 1): four entries now, and all four say the same thing from different sides - **the geometric quantity a probe reads off a checkpoint is an output of the objective that trained it, and its sign is known in advance once you know what the loss asked for.** Mechanical rule for us: **when our white-box model scores an input by norm, prototype distance, or margin, we state which training term created that quantity and its expected sign, and where that term is present the score is reported as a readout of the objective rather than as evidence about the input - because a gap that a one-line mask in the loss produced will be found by any probe, and finding it tells us nothing about the model's uncertainty.**
- **Pattern:** **The paper's only honest table is the one that separates seen from unseen, and it shows the generalisation term earning its keep exactly where its name says it should and losing slightly where it does not - the seen/unseen split is the cheap version of run 730's "was the phenomenon expressible" check.** Table 3: no SNA losses gives seen 88.6 / unseen 76.2; supervised alignment alone (IA+PA) gives seen 91.5 / unseen 77.7; adding the unlabelled non-alignment term takes unseen to 79.1 but seen *down* to 91.2. That single split carries the paper's entire claim, and it is also the only place where a term is allowed to cost something. Everywhere else the metric is "overall AUC", an unweighted mean over six to nine OOD sets, which cannot show a trade because a trade averaged against itself looks additive. Note against run 735 (a paper with no task metric at all, where the absence had to be stated outright), run 725 (a bound whose failing constant was estimated rather than argued away) and run 730 (a negative result repaired by measuring base rates): **the folder now has four consecutive entries where the useful number is a two-column split rather than a headline average.** Mechanical rule for us: **any claim about generalising to a held-out or unseen family is reported as a seen column and an unseen column with the trade visible, never as their mean - because a mean over the two is the one summary in which a term that helps only on new data and hurts slightly on old data cannot be distinguished from a term that does nothing.**
- **Pattern:** **The ablation tables are point estimates in a paper whose main table prints a standard deviation larger than the ablation differences, so the three ablations that carry the method's story are all inside the paper's own noise - and this run's own caution applies to its own entry, which is why the numbers above are quoted as measured rather than as findings.** Table 1 reports CIFAR-100 80/20-25 AUC at 82.8 with +/-3.2; Table 2 compares the pull-only cosine variant to the full loss at 79.4 vs 80.8 in the same configuration with no variance given; Table 5's nonlinearity gain is 1.7 overall / 2.0 unseen; Table 3's three comparisons are 0.3 to 1.4 points with no variance. The one effect far outside all of that is the detector half of the gate (threshold 0 costs 14.4 AUC points on CIFAR-10 6/4-50), and it is the least discussed number in the paper. Read against run 733 (a headline 0.0117 against a seed spread of 0.0085, with the five-seed study run on the losing variant), run 731 (a deficit table whose summary hid the losing cells) and run 729 (a correlation over surviving seeds): **five entries now, and the defect has migrated from "which seeds survived" to "which differences anyone would believe".** Mechanical rule for us: **an ablation difference we publish carries the variance of the configuration it is a difference between, drawn from the paper's own table where one exists, and if no variance is printed for that cell the difference is labelled unresolved rather than reported as a gain - because a 0.7-point gap inside a +/-3.2 seed spread is not a result, and treating it as one is the single most common way this literature produces findings that do not replicate.**

- **Done:** arxiv-2611.07753 - Neural Collapse on Imbalanced Datasets and Open-Set Scenario (read full v1 HTML, 281,680 chars / 6,189 lines: abstract, §1-§5, Theorems 1-4 with Eq. 6-23 in full including the BCE bias quadratics, Tables 1-4 reconstructed from the doubled-maths cells, §4.1-§4.3 reporting prose in full, Table S.1 training settings, §S.1.1; the appendix proofs S.2-S.4 were **not** read beyond the statements they are cited from. The paper has **no Limitations section** and no task metric - no accuracy, no AUROC, no detection number - so the entry says so outright rather than implying an evaluation exists)
- **Pattern:** **The folder now has the answer to the open-set half of the collapse question, and it is a warning rather than a tool: a classifier vector with zero training samples behind it is not idle, it converges to a specific direction with the same confidence and the same tiny variance as a trained one.** Theorems 2 and 3 say that with K₁ populated classes and K−K₁ empty ones, the empty vectors all coincide and point along minus the global feature mean, orthogonal to the trained weights; empirically they reach about 0.999-1.000 mutual similarity by epoch 50, i.e. *before* the known classes have settled. Read against run 734 (self-duality and weight equiangularity carry the OOD-detector prediction, so the head rather than the feature cloud is the object of interest) and run 733 (compaction moved the spread ratio without moving AUROC in the direction theory wanted), the distinction is now usable: those entries argued that head geometry matters; this one says head geometry is *generated* by support, and where support is zero the geometry is generated by something else entirely. Mechanical rule for us: **when we read prototypes or classifier rows out of a model and treat a low-support or zero-support direction as "unused", we first measure the gram matrix among such directions and their angle to the global feature mean - because the theory here says empty directions collapse onto one another and look as crisp as trained ones, and a crispness statistic computed without checking for that collapse cannot tell a real class from a placeholder.**
- **Pattern:** **The load-bearing variable is the loss's normalisation, and the paper's whole result is that changing it from per-sample to per-class is what converts a distorted geometry into the standard one - which makes normalisation a confound in every cross-model geometry comparison the folder has run.** Per-class averaging is algebraically inverse-frequency weighting, and at IF=100 the globally averaged objective prints class frequency straight into the similarity matrix (off-diagonals grading from about −0.3 in the head classes to about 0.1 in the tail, a span of roughly 0.4) while the class-wise version stays inside a band under 0.024. The BCE bias is the cleanest confirmation available in this literature: 4.663-4.669 measured against 4.6698 predicted on CIFAR10-LT across four imbalance factors, and 6.328-6.332 against 6.3437 on CIFAR100-LT, on two architectures - agreement that a free parameter cannot fake. Compare run 731 (a bound repaired by adding a reciprocal-density term) and run 732 (a score whose tuned k degenerated to 1): three entries now in which the difference between two configurations is a single algebraic term in the objective rather than an architectural or data choice. Mechanical rule for us: **any claim we make that two checkpoints differ in geometry, collapse, or prototype quality carries the loss normalisation of each, and where the normalisations differ we treat the geometry difference as attributable to the loss until a matched-normalisation comparison says otherwise - because per-class and per-sample averaging are the same architecture with different objectives, and no measured similarity can distinguish them afterwards.**
- **Pattern:** **The one prediction the paper reports failing is a conditioning failure rather than a refutation, and it is the folder's cleanest example of a theorem whose residual is numerical - the score you want is a direction of a vector whose norm is going to zero.** The empty-class vector should sit exactly antiparallel to the global feature mean, and at the theoretical minimum it does; but as the mean norm collapses toward zero any perturbation reorients it, so the measured cosine wanders (−0.6181 for BCE at epoch 600, and −0.2504 → −0.0492 → −0.0442 across CE stages in Table 3) while every other statistic in the same table is within noise of its constant. The paper states the cause plainly and leaves the quantity in the table rather than dropping it. Read against run 729 (a correlation computed on surviving seeds) and run 727 (a score whose invariance group was proved before its diagnostic value was claimed): the difference is that those papers' numbers were compromised by which samples survived, while this number is compromised by the arithmetic of a normalised angle near the origin. Mechanical rule for us: **a normalised direction or cosine statistic we publish is accompanied by the norm of the vector it was computed from, and where that norm falls below the scale at which the direction is stable we report the instability rather than the angle - because a ratio of two small quantities is a measurement of noise, and an error term that is a conditioning problem is fixed by changing the statistic, not by adding data.**

Two searches were run.
