- **Done:** arxiv-2505.06708 - Gated Attention for Large Language Models: Non-linearity, Sparsity, and Attention-Sink-Free (read full text v1: abstract, §1-§6 including the limitations paragraph, §5 related work, Appendix A.1-A.5 in full; Tables 1-6 as cells; Figures 1-6 read from captions and the prose that reports them, not inspected as images; reference list not read beyond resolving works cited in §5)
- **Pattern:** **A component published inside a bundle may not be the part doing the work, and this paper's Appendix A.1 is the cleanest self-inflicted demonstration in the head.** The prior work this paper positions against was named for routing — a sigmoid that selects the top-k among pools of key/value/output experts — and when the authors collapse the selection to a single expert, deleting the routing mechanism entirely and leaving only the sigmoid on the value output, that single-expert configuration is the best row in the whole switch-head family on both perplexity (5.808) and benchmarks, while the largest routing configuration (8 experts, top-8) is worse and costs 38M parameters against 3M. Note the shape of the move: instead of arguing that routing matters less than reported, they remove it and re-measure the same family, which converts a bundled claim into a single-component claim at the cost of one training run per row. Mechanical rule for us: **when we attribute an effect to a selection, routing or competition mechanism, we rerun the comparison with the selection collapsed to a single option — same gate, same slots, no choice — and report that row beside the full mechanism, because a gate that survives its own routing being deleted is the gate that was the mechanism, and everything we would have said about "specialisation" belongs to the routing we just removed.** Cross-reference run 705 (a mechanism and a scale knob are indistinguishable unless the selective part is replaced by its own average - the same collapse of a selector, from the other side), run 704 (a unit whose write is near-zero is the load-bearing unit; here a module whose selection never selects is).
- **Pattern:** **Two named factors in one architectural addition can be separated by substituting, at the same position, a cheaper component that supplies only one of them - and that is what the RMSNorm row and the non-sparse-sigmoid row do here.** The gate was credited with two things: a non-linearity in a path that the algebra shows collapses into one low-rank map (value projection composed with output projection, further narrowed by grouped-query attention), and an input-dependent sparse score. A parameter-free RMSNorm at the identical position supplies only the non-linearity and still cuts average perplexity from 6.026 to 5.847, while a rescaled sigmoid confined to [0.5, 1.0] supplies only the non-linearity-and-gate-shape without the small scores and gives 5.900; but only the genuinely sparse, query-dependent gate drops the first-token attention share from 0.467 to 0.048, while the non-sparse gate leaves it at 0.451. So the perplexity gain and the sink suppression are carried by different halves, and neither half alone reproduces the other's effect. Mechanical rule for us: **when we credit a component with more than one job, we name each job and exhibit one substitute that supplies that job alone — in the head we already do this for "does this direction do anything" and "is this a scale artefact", but not yet for multi-purpose components — because a single ablation that removes the component removes all its jobs at once, and reports the sum as though it were the mechanism.** Cross-reference run 706 (variance versus magnitude separated by a norm-matched control), run 707 (magnitude and direction separated by three ratios before any conclusion), run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget - here the rival null is a parameter-free normaliser).

The **line-based slicer `/tmp/lit/ln.py <start> <end>`** (the default for heavy-maths documents since run 704) was used throughout and was the right call here too: every displayed equation arrives as a TeX line followed by a numbered `(N)` line and a typeset line, so the three-part block would break any character-offset slicing. - **Done:** arxiv-2409.04431 - Theory, Analysis, and Best Practices for Sigmoid Self-Attention (read full text v1: abstract, §1-§7 including the conclusion, Appendix A limitations in full, Appendix E bias-term derivation including Proposition E.1 and its proof, Appendix G.1.2 bias ablation and G.2.1-G.2.4 vision ablations, plus the ASR findings list in §5.4; Appendices C, D and F (UAP proof, Jacobian proof, kernel details) and most of Appendix G located by heading and read only as far as their statements; every figure-borne magnitude taken from a caption or a prose sentence, never from a plotted value)
- **Pattern:** **A removed normaliser's functional role can be *restored analytically* by solving for one parameter, and this entry is the head's cleanest case of a hyperparameter that was derived rather than tuned.** Softmax supplied two things the elementwise sigmoid does not: per-element mass of about 1/n, and a specific gradient scale with respect to a logit. Requiring the sigmoid gates to sum to one yields a single solving bias bounded between −log(n−1)−M and −log(n−1)−m (Proposition E.1), and requiring the gate's derivative to match softmax's yields the same order −log n; the empirical sweep over constant biases then peaks near −10 and degrades at −15, which is the same order for the sequence lengths used. Mechanical rule for us: **whenever our white-box model replaces or drops a component of the network, we state the property that component supplied, solve for the parameter that restores it, and report the solved value beside the empirically best one - because a replacement whose only tuning is a sweep has not shown what the original component was for, and a derived value that lands near the sweep's optimum is evidence the explanation of the component is right.** Cross-reference run 707 (the intermediate quantity that transmits a training signal, named rather than correlated), run 704 (a bound that predicts the direction of an effect before it is measured).
- **Pattern:** **A stability claim is cheaper and more informative when it is a *curve over a sweep* rather than a point at one setting - and the LR-sensitivity score here is the reusable form of that.** The score is the expected excess loss over a learning-rate grid relative to the best learning rate on that grid, so a flat score means the configuration tolerates a wrong learning rate; the paper reads every ablation through it (LayerScale on/off, QK norm on/off) and treats "no spikes in gradient norm" with smooth loss as a success criterion in its own right. The same discipline appears in the bias ablation as a non-monotone curve with an interior optimum, which no single-setting comparison could have revealed. Mechanical rule for us: **any intervention or architectural variant we evaluate is reported as a curve over its own strength or over the learning rate, with the summary statistic stated (here an expected excess over the best point), and a single-setting success is not counted as a robustness result - because the interesting failure in this paper is at the *ends* of the range, and a sweep midpoint would have looked like a clean win.** Cross-reference run 703 (the plateau-then-cliff curve, where a correlation was being read off the flat part), run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget).
- **Pattern:** **The failure that cost prior work its result was a *scale at step zero*, invisible in final accuracy - and this paper's whole contribution is finding it by looking at an initialisation statistic.** Naive sigmoid attention was reported to work, did not reproduce, and the diagnosis was the initial attention output norm being large, fixed by moving initial logit mass into the sigmoid's near-zero region (ALiBi, or the −log n bias). Note what the fix has in common across the two routes: both reduce a norm at initialisation while leaving the trained function class alone, and the paper's own FLOP accounting shows the arithmetic difference between the two activations is negligible, so the entire gap was never about the activation's cost or capacity. Mechanical rule for us: **when a mechanism or a read-out "does not transfer" between two models, the first diagnostic is a scale statistic measured at initialisation or at the start of the fit, printed for both models - because a difference in initial geometry produces a difference in what the fit later finds, and an end-state comparison will attribute that difference to the mechanism under study.** Cross-reference run 707 (a normalisation that makes magnitude and direction inseparable, separated by ratios), run 706 (variance versus magnitude separated by a norm-matched control), run 701/702 (the offset ratio, the same statistic one level up: what the layer is fed decides what it learns).
- **Could not read:** nothing failed twice and nothing was abandoned. **Selection.** The first carried candidate from run 707 - `arxiv-2409.04431`, named there by id and title - was **free**, and the dedupe gate printed all three checks in one command before any fetch: `-f` reported free for both `aether/arxiv-2409.04431.md` and `core/` (no such file), `grep -c "^arxiv-2409.04431 |"` returned **0** on both INDEX files, and the lowercased title substring (`sigmoid self-attention`) returned **0** on both INDEX bodies. **Discovery and tooling note, new and worth keeping:** the v1 HTML fetched clean on the **first attempt** - 1,171,087 raw bytes, **209,425 chars** after tag strip, the largest document in the recent head (runs 704-707 were 59k-131k) because it carries four proof appendices and an extensive experiment appendix. The rendering quirk that matters: **`grep -n` line numbers and character offsets diverge sharply** because each displayed equation occupies a short line while the TeX restatement is a very long one, so slicing by character offset from a grep line number lands in the wrong place - the run's first two offset slices both returned the same inline footnote fragment ("...ble at https://github.com/apple/ml-sigmoid-attention") and looked like truncated sections. **Fix: switched to a line-based slicer (`/tmp/lit/ln.py <start> <end>`) that prints `lineNo|text`, so grep -n line numbers index directly into the document.** This will recur on every paper with heavy display maths and should be the default for such documents. - **Done:** arxiv-2603.17771 - Attention Sinks Induce Gradient Sinks (read full text v1: abstract, §1-§6 including the limitations paragraph, both theorem statements with their hypotheses, the V-scale Jacobian eigenvalues, all five figure captions plus the prose that reports what they show; figures not inspected as images; reference list read only as far as resolving the works cited in §§1-5)
- **Pattern:** **A backward quantity can be *read off* the forward pass when the two are tied by an exact identity, and this entry is the head's first case where the control parameter for a training signal is a number we already compute - the attention column.** The value-state gradient of a token is literally the attention column times the upstream output gradients, so the same column mass that makes a token a forward sink is the multiplier on its backward pressure; the expectation bound then makes that quantitative (squared gradient norm scales with the squared column mass plus a noise term). That is the structure our white-box model wants: not a correlation between a forward statistic and a training effect, but a named intermediate quantity that transmits between them and can be intervened on directly. Mechanical rule for us: **when we claim the optimiser reacted to some structure, we name the intermediate quantity that transmits the reaction, exhibit an identity or bound linking it to a forward-readable statistic, and intervene on the intermediate - because a claim that rests only on the two observables co-occurring cannot distinguish a cause from a shared consequence.** Cross-reference run 704 (the path-product bound, a forward-computable surrogate for a sensitivity claim - the same move one level up), run 706 (the same sink traced link by link down to named weight objects).
- **Pattern:** **A normalisation that attenuates in proportion to magnitude makes magnitude and direction structurally inseparable, so an ablation that changes one is always reportable as changing the other - and this entry's fix is to split the effect into three ratios before drawing any conclusion.** Bloat (gradient on the normed input over gradient on the branch residual) is large on the sink token; compress (branch gradient over normed-input gradient) tracks activation scale with slope close to minus one; change (block input over block output) stays near unity. Only the third is what the residual path sees, and it is the one that would have been reported as "the gradient is huge" if only an end-to-end norm had been measured. Mechanical rule for us: **any intervention or ablation we run across a normalisation boundary is reported as the triple (pre-normalisation magnitude, normaliser attenuation, net change at the residual), and a claim of selectivity is only made on the middle term - because in a pre-norm stack a big norm and a big directional effect are the same measurement unless the ratios separate them.** Cross-reference run 706 (variance is not magnitude, separated by a norm-matched control), run 705 (a mechanism and a scale knob are indistinguishable unless the selective part is replaced by its own average).
- **Pattern:** **A causal claim is earned by inventing a third object that changes one link of a chain and leaves the others alone - and here that object is a *radial* map chosen to be near-identity exactly where the forward pass matters.** V-scale multiplies each value by r/(r+C), so it contracts small-norm values (where the gradient intervention is strongest) and leaves large-norm values almost untouched (where the forward computation actually lives), which is why the model can keep its sink while losing the activation. Note what the intervention does not do: it removes only the dominant channel, so the mechanism claim is explicitly partial and the authors say so. Mechanical rule for us: **a mechanistic intervention we publish is designed so that its forward effect and its backward effect are separated by construction (a map that acts on norms, gates on a scalar, freezes a branch), and its partiality is stated in the same sentence as the claim - because an end-to-end perturbation that changes everything at once supports only a co-occurrence story.** Cross-reference run 705 (a conditional bound tested with fixed upstream gradients), run 700 (reconstruction and interpretability separated by freezing one branch).

- **Done:** arxiv-2605.06611 - The Structural Origin of Attention Sink: Variance Discrepancy, Super Neurons, and Dimension Disparity (read full text v1: abstract, §1-§6 including the limitations paragraph, Appendices A-D; Tables 1-4 as cells; figures 1-23 read from captions and the prose describing them, not inspected as images; the reference list skimmed only to resolve the works cited in §§1-6)
- **Pattern:** **A dominant representation direction can be traced down to named weight objects, and this entry is the folder's only full-path account of that - which is the missing half of run 705's spectral story.** The chain is measured link by link on one model: causal masking leaves position 0 unaveraged, so its dimension-wise variance stays high after layer-1 value aggregation; the output projection is biased to carry it (mean Kendall tau 0.32 between column weights and first-token input variance); a handful of huge-norm FFN gate/up columns are gate-open and massively active only for token 0; one of those rows in the down-projection is heavy-tailed and pushes the activation into a single coordinate; after the next layer's RMSNorm the outlier coordinate is 1.2568 against a mean absolute value of 0.0048, so the token has collapsed to a near-basis vector; and heads whose sink key aligns with the query matrix's leading singular direction then score positive on almost every position. Mechanical rule for us: **when we report that a representation direction is dominant, we name the smallest set of weight objects that produce it and give a measured statistic for each link, and we predict the intervention consequences of that chain rather than only correlating one end with the other - because run 705 could say the mode exists and how a gate contracts it, and only a link-by-link trace says which weights a prediction has to be conditioned on.** Cross-reference run 705 (the same sink as a low-rank spectral spike above a random-matrix edge), run 704 (the same sink as an over-mixing brake with a path-product bound).
- **Pattern:** **Variance is not magnitude, and this entry buys the distinction with a norm-matched control - the cleanest version in the head of a null that isolates the claimed quantity from the trivial one beside it.** Mean-centred rescaling of one token's aggregated output by a factor lambda turns that token into a dominant sink that grows with lambda; scaling the same vector by the same factor without centring does not produce a sink at all, even though it inflates the query-key dot product more directly. The same discipline shows up twice more: the variance measurement is run on random tokens so a fixed BOS cannot manufacture zero variance at position 0, and the early-layer outlier is separated from ordinary norm growth by a dominance ratio (max absolute coordinate over mean absolute coordinate) rather than by norm. Mechanical rule for us: **any claim that a statistic other than raw magnitude drives a behaviour comes with a control that holds magnitude fixed and varies only the claimed statistic - because in a residual stream where norms grow with depth, an inflated norm will usually reproduce the effect you thought you were attributing to structure.** Cross-reference run 705 (the random-matrix and random-subspace nulls), run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget), run 704 (the min-norm value write that is a function, not an inactive unit).

- **Done:** arxiv-2611.20403 - Attention Sinks as Spectral Spikes: A Mechanism Analysis of Gated Attention (read full text v1: abstract, §1-§7, Appendices D.1-D.8, E.1-E.3, F.1-F.4, G.1-G.3 and the limitations appendix H; Tables 1-14 as cells; figures 1-9 read from captions and the prose describing them, not inspected as images; Appendices A, B, C, I located but not read for algebra - only the statements of Theorem 3.1, Proposition 3.2, Theorem 4.1 and Corollary 4.2)
- **Pattern:** **A dominant direction in a representation is only a finding if it is scored against a null that has the same shape - and this entry is the folder's cleanest worked example because the null is a random-matrix noise edge, not a shuffled control.** Every spectral claim in the paper is reported as a distance above a Marchenko-Pastur bulk edge computed from the same sequence length and width, and every alignment claim is reported against a random k/T subspace baseline; the sink subspace clears that baseline by 20-36x, which is what makes "the leading mode is sink-aligned" more than an artefact of picking the leading eigenvector of a noisy matrix. The sink-subspace projection is the second half of the null: removing the claimed subspace drops the leading eigenvalue by 12-19 percent, so the claim is checked by subtraction as well as by correlation. Mechanical rule for us: **every low-rank, dominant-direction or subspace claim we publish carries (i) a random-matrix or random-subspace baseline computed from the same shapes and sample size, and (ii) the value of the statistic after projecting the claimed subspace out - because leading eigenvectors of noisy covariance matrices always look structured, and only the gap above the edge plus the projection test say otherwise.** Cross-reference run 696 (the shuffled null that beat every activation-only ranking), run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget).
- **Pattern:** **A mechanism and a scale knob are indistinguishable unless the selective part of the mechanism is replaced by its own average - and the pair of numbers here shows how easily the same gate reads as flat or as strongly selective depending on where the statistic lives.** The elementwise gate and its per-token feature average both shrink the output by roughly the same amount, so in raw activation energy retention the two are identical (0.047 vs 0.044 for strongly vs weakly sink-aligned directions). In the feature-standardised covariance the same comparison is 0.740 vs 0.945, and the gap collapses to 0.957 vs 0.990 once the gate is averaged - so the selectivity is real, but it was invisible in the unstandardised statistic. Mechanical rule for us: **any claim that a component suppresses or selects a particular structure is paired with an ablation in which the component is replaced by its own mean or scalar summary, and the effect is reported in the normalisation where it is claimed to live plus the raw scale - because a mechanism that only exists in one normalisation is a statement about the normalisation, and a uniform shrinkage can pass for selectivity in the wrong geometry.** Cross-reference run 699 (dictionary latents positioned by chat-format boilerplate - the same non-semantic variance), run 700 (the discarded component named rather than hidden).
- **Pattern:** **A local Jacobian bound is worth publishing only when it is probed with a counterfactual that holds the expensive half of the pass fixed - and this paper's fixed-upstream-gradient recomputation is the cheapest version of that recipe in the head.** The gradient claim is conditional on fixed attention weights and fixed incoming gradients, so instead of an end-to-end ablation they cache the upstream gradients from the real gated backward pass, recompute the sink-column contribution with the gate removed, and compare - yielding an attenuation ratio well below one that tracks a forward-computable proxy across layers. The same construction separates "the gate acts" from "the model trained differently": the trained gated model with the gate switched off still shows first-token gradient amplification above the baseline scale, so the active gate contributes attenuation that the learned weights did not compensate for. Mechanical rule for us: **a bound or surrogate that is stated conditionally is tested conditionally - hold the conditioning quantities fixed, toggle only the component under test, and report both the conditional ratio and the end-to-end trained comparison side by side - because an end-to-end difference confounds the mechanism with everything training did in response to it.** Cross-reference run 704 (the path-product bound used as a pre-ablation surrogate), run 700 (reconstruction versus interpretability separated by freezing one branch).

- **Done:** arxiv-2504.02732 - Why do LLMs attend to the first token? (read full text v4: abstract, §1-§6, Appendices A-D including both proofs, Tables 1-3 as cells; figures 1-10 not inspected as images)
- **Pattern:** **The first entry in this folder to give a *path-product bound* that maps one attention weight monotonically onto one predicted sensitivity, which is the analytic surrogate our white-box model needs before it can call an ablation result predicted rather than post-hoc.** Extending prior over-squashing work to multiple heads, the norm of the Jacobian from an input token's representation to an output token's representation is bounded by a sum over attribution paths of a product of head-averaged attention coefficients; each factor is a number we can read off a forward pass, so lowering one weight path multiplicatively lowers the bound for every path through it. That turns "does this component matter" into a computable quantity with a stated direction, and the empirical shape matches it on all four axes the bound carries (depth, width via head count, context length, and packing). Mechanical rule for us: **every circuit or component we publish ships with a path-product bound computed from quantities available in one forward pass, and the intervention test is reported as agreement or disagreement with that bound's predicted direction - because a post-hoc ablation that happens to hurt is not a prediction, and the gap between the bound and the observed effect is itself the finding.** Cross-reference run 696 (a per-unit worth is a property of the ablated set, not the unit), run 701 (measure the input side before drawing a conclusion about the mechanism).
- **Pattern:** **A unit whose write is near-zero is not an inactive unit - here it is the most load-bearing unit in the block, and the paper shows it in a single worked head.** The apostrophe head in Gemma 7B layer 1 fires sharply when the previous token is an apostrophe and otherwise parks its attention on the first token, whose value vector has the smallest norm in the sequence; attending to a small value makes the attention output small, so the block contributes almost nothing and the residual pathway carries the information forward. That is a learned if-else whose else-branch is silence, and it is the same phenomenon that makes heads look "dead" on every activity ranking in this folder. Compare run 696, where a shuffled null beat every activation-only per-neuron ranking, and run 701/702, where dead-unit counts turned out to measure input geometry. Mechanical rule for us: **any per-unit activity or importance statistic we report is accompanied by the norm of what the unit WRITES (value norm, decoder-direction norm times activation) and by a test of whether its low score coincides with a deliberate no-op - because a unit that writes nothing on purpose and a unit that learned nothing are identical on every activity metric we currently print.** Cross-reference run 696 (the ranking beaten by a shuffled null), run 700 (the discarded component named rather than hidden).
- **Pattern:** **Removing a structural component does not degrade a model, it breaks it - and the failure is concentrated exactly where the theory says the component is for, which is both the strongest confirmation in the head and its sharpest warning about ablation claims.** Deleting the first token from Gemma 7B at inference takes ARC-e from 80.77 to 28.49 and RULER at 4096 context from 82.57 to **0.00**, while the same removal costs nothing when the model never learned to rely on that token in the first place (pre-training without a fixed beginning token leaves the sink on whatever token is first). The pre-training ablation is the control that makes this a mechanism claim: sink formation is inevitable, but *which* token carries it is a choice the training pipeline makes, and the two regimes fail in different ways. Mechanical rule for us: **when we ablate a structural component we (i) report the damage on the sub-task the mechanism is supposed to serve, not only the aggregate, and (ii) include a variant trained or configured without that component to show the damage is dependence rather than generic breakage - because an aggregate score hides the zero, and a catastrophic drop under ablation is evidence of reliance, not of necessity.** Cross-reference run 697 (decompose a drop to the sub-task that moved), run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget - here the null is a model that never formed the habit).

- **Done:** arxiv-2605.17659 - Bug or Feature 2: Weight Drift and Activation Sparsity (read full text v3: abstract, §1-§10 including related work, both theorems and the worked proof in §2, all of §4-§7 and the limitations in §8; Tables 1-3, 9, 10, 11 as cells; appendix B.3.2 read in full and B.4 read for its numbers; figures 1-7, 9, 10, 13, 15, 16 **not** inspected as images; appendices A.1-A.4, C, D located but not read in full)
- **Pattern:** **The sparsity and the sign of the weight distribution in a ReLU MLP block are set by a gradient bias that exists before any data-specific learning - so a "silent unit" may be reporting the optimiser, not a feature, and this is the mechanism behind the offset cluster of runs 701 and 702.** The proof is two steps: conditioning two random downstream weight rows on both surviving a ReLU gate forces them to share a positive component along the input direction, so their expected inner product is non-negative; then under MSE or softmax cross-entropy the expected gradient with respect to a positive pre-activation is non-negative, so gradient descent steps the weights negative, which pushes pre-activations negative, which the ReLU maps to zero - a closed loop. It appears on completely random Gaussian inputs, so the data cannot be the cause, and it is broken only by mean-centring normalisation (ResNet with batch norm is the sole configuration near 43% negative pre-activations against 60-80% everywhere else). Mechanical rule for us: **before attributing a low firing rate, a near-zero activation mean or a high activation sparsity to a semantic cause, report the mean of the downstream weight matrix and the fraction of negative pre-activations for that layer, and check whether the pattern survives subtracting a constant interlayer from frozen activations - because a statistic that moves when we subtract a constant is a statement about input geometry and optimisation, not about what the model learned.** Cross-reference run 701 and run 702 (the offset / mean-over-spread ratio, the same quantity one level upstream), run 699 (latents positioned by chat-format boilerplate rather than meaning): four consecutive entries, four levels, one lesson.
- **Pattern:** **A structural property can be shown harmless without being shown useful, and the way to do it is to force the property to a range of values with a mechanism that is not the model's own - this paper's Percentile Centering versus Top-K pair is the cheapest worked version in the head.** Percentile Centering is a layer-norm variant that subtracts a quantile of the pre-activation instead of the mean, so a chosen fraction falls below zero before the ReLU; Top-K is applied to GELU because ReLU's drift sets a floor that cannot be pushed lower. Sweeping either gives a plateau to roughly 70% sparsity then a cliff, and adding the mechanism label to the fitted curve moves R² only from 0.565 to 0.618, so the *level* is what matters and the *mechanism* barely does. Mechanical rule for us: **when we report that a structural property of a model (sparsity, rank, dictionary utilisation) correlates with or predicts capability, we also report the curve obtained by forcing that property across a range with at least one mechanism other than the model's own - because a correlation with an emergent property is not evidence the property does useful work, and a plateau-then-cliff shape means our correlation may be being read off the plateau where it carries almost no information.** Cross-reference run 695 (the construction baseline that made a score interpretable) and run 700 (a gain needs a null that spends the same budget).
- **Pattern:** **A post-hoc component swap at fixed weights separates "this component improved the read-out" from "this component disclosed something already present" - and here the swap is one epoch and it reverses the architecture recommendation.** ReLUfication trains with GELU and fine-tunes to ReLU for a single epoch: post-activation sparsity goes to 55-74% while the negative pre-activation fraction barely moves, so the conversion is not creating the near-zero region, it is truncating a region the drift had already built; accuracy drops by under two points and not at all on some models. The other half of the same lesson is the failure: ReLU-squared wins on the language model but collapses to chance on an unnormalised MLP and on ResNet with batch norm, so an activation's benefit is a property of the normalisation it was paired with, not of the activation. Mechanical rule for us: **wherever we can swap a component of a fitted read-out post hoc while holding everything upstream fixed, we do, and we report the pre-swap value beside the post-swap one - because a component that "improves" a read-out may only be disclosing structure that was already in the weights, and a component whose benefit disappears under a different normalisation was never the cause.** Cross-reference run 700 (reconstruction versus interpretability separated by freezing one branch) and run 696 (the construction baseline that validated a score).

- **Done:** arxiv-2605.31518 - On the Relationship Between Activation Outliers and Feature Death in Sparse Autoencoders (read full text v1: abstract, sections 1-8, all six main figure captions, Tables 1 and S7 as cells, appendices A-E located and skimmed by heading and by the passages the main text cites; figures 1-6 and supplementary figures not inspected as images)
- **Pattern:** **A dead-or-live count is a property of the activation distribution's geometry, not of the dictionary - and this is the paper run 701's entry was building on, so the two should always be read as one mechanism with two settings.** The load-bearing quantity is the norm of the activation mean over the norm of the per-token spread; when it is large, each feature's pre-activation is dominated by a constant shift (the weight dotted with the mean) and the input-dependent signal barely moves it, so a feature whose shift is negative can never fire and one that wins the top-k competition never loses it. The closed forms need only the ratio and the sample count, and they hold across 454 model-layer combinations with rank correlations 0.82 and 0.89 and no fitting. Mechanical rule for us: **every dictionary, probe or read-out we fit on frozen activations carries this ratio for its input layer, measured after per-token normalisation, printed beside the dead or live fraction - because otherwise two dictionaries trained on two models can differ for a reason that has nothing to do with the models.** Cross-reference run 701 (the same ratio re-derived for a frozen random encoder and for reinforcement-learning agents), run 699 (dictionary latents positioned by chat-format boilerplate rather than meaning) and run 696 (a partial dictionary's coverage is a property of the pipeline).
- **Pattern:** **Two failure modes with one shared cause but different recovery timescales, and separating them is what turns a hyperparameter result into a mechanism.** Negative-shift death is permanent-ish because the only parameter that can lift it is the bias, which learns the mean slowly and slower the larger the mean (99 percent of it inside 200k steps at a ratio below 5, 50-70 percent after two million steps at 30 or more, directly proportional to the ratio); competition death recovers in about 200k steps because living features shrink and free slots. The existing auxiliary-loss mitigation is then re-explained: it helps by preventing *new* negative-shift deaths created during that shrinkage, not by reviving old ones, so its benefit shrinks as the ratio grows - which is exactly the observed pattern and is invisible if you only report the total dead count. Mechanical rule for us: **when we report a failure rate under an intervention, we break it into the sub-populations that share a cause but differ in recovery path, and we put each mitigation beside the pathway it actually acts on - because a flat total can hide active turnover in both directions, which is also the warning that a dead-feature count sampled at one step is not a stable statistic.** Cross-reference run 701 (the offset measured on the input side before any conclusion about the mechanism), run 697 (a statistic of an average is not the average of the statistic) and run 695 (the construction baseline that made a score interpretable).
- **Pattern:** **A preprocessing step the field already uses inconsistently is given a decision rule - measure one statistic, and if it is high the step is mandatory - and the paper then shows the cheap version of the step can itself be poisoned by the outliers it is meant to remove.** The rule is the ratio (centre when high) plus an effective-rank of the post-normalised covariance (whiten when low-rank, below about two percent of the hidden width). Centring by the arithmetic mean fails on four of nineteen models because a few extreme tokens pull the mean away from the typical token (angle between the two mean estimates 0.29-0.72 cosine), and the geometric median fixes it with at most 2.3 percent death; on synthetic data centring moves the mean maximum cosine between learned and true directions from 0.38 to 0.97, and on a protein model a centred 2048-feature dictionary recovers more annotated concepts (100 of 187) than an uncentred 8192-feature one (73). Mechanical rule for us: **any preprocessing or normalisation we apply before interpreting is published with the statistic that says whether it was necessary and a robust-location variant, and any comparison against a bigger unprocessed baseline is reported at equal or smaller budget - because "we cleaned the inputs" is otherwise indistinguishable from "we spent more capacity".** Cross-reference run 700 (the discarded residual component named rather than hidden) and run 698 (agreement between two read-outs of one forward pass cannot separate representation from input-side structure).

- **Done:** arxiv-2611.29752 - Why Units Die in the Layer After a Frozen Random Encoder, and What the Number of Live Units Measures (read full text v1: abstract, sections 1-9 including the discussion, all five tables as cells, all seven figure captions; figures 1-7 not inspected as images)
- **Pattern:** **A count of live, dead or dormant units is a function of the input distribution's geometry, not of the model or the task - and this paper is the head's cleanest worked demonstration because the author retracts his own metric with it.** The load-bearing quantity is an offset: the length of the mean input vector divided by the square root of total variance, large when the layer's inputs form a tight cluster far from the origin. A randomly placed ReLU boundary that misses such a cluster puts the unit on or off for *every* example, and a unit off for every example gets no gradient and stays off. Holding the game, the encoder and the training fixed and moving only the offset took Pong from 3-6 live units of 64 to 59-63, while a frozen encoder on unaltered screens - and the same author's earlier paper - read the ordering of these counts across games as an ordering of task complexity. Mechanical rule for us: **every unit-level statistic we publish (live units, dead features, firing rate, dictionary utilisation) is accompanied by the offset or an equivalent mean-magnitude-over-spread number for that layer's inputs, computed on the same data with the same rule - because otherwise a difference between two models may be nothing but the geometry of what was fed into the layer.** Cross-reference run 696 (a per-unit worth is a property of the ablated set, not the unit) and run 699 (dictionary latents positioned by chat-format boilerplate rather than meaning): the same lesson at three different levels, that the unit's reading is co-determined by its inputs.
- **Pattern:** **The cheapest pre-training diagnostic in the head is now a single normalised ratio over the inputs of any layer we are about to train, and it replaces a downstream explanation with an upstream measurement.** Because a random encoder multiplies patches by signs of either sign, it preserves the energy of the average input and of the deviations from it alike, so it passes the offset through nearly unchanged (33 conditions, rank correlation 0.988), and an analytic formula needing only a sample of inputs and the layer sizes predicts the median offset of the encoder's output to within 25 percent in all 44 conditions with a median error of 1.4 percent, without drawing a single weight. That makes the offset usable *before* building a front end - our case, where probes and SAEs are trained on fixed activations we cannot change. Mechanical rule for us: **before training any read-out layer on frozen features, run one pass that reports the mean-over-spread ratio of those features and, if it is large, first try subtracting the training-set mean; and when an ablation or probe surprisingly fails per-prompt, check this ratio before concluding the concept is absent - because units born off never see the gradient we assume they receive.** Cross-reference run 695 (the construction baseline that made a score interpretable) and run 700 (the discarded residual component named rather than hidden): both are the same habit of measuring the input side before drawing a conclusion about the mechanism.
- **Pattern:** **Two quantities that both explain the same structural outcome, with one much better evidenced than the other, and the weaker one is reported as weaker throughout - which is the honesty template this folder needs for its own multivariate story.** The offset is the strong factor: it moves with the count in Breakout, Space Invaders, image classifiers, a reservoir computer (rank correlation -0.908) and GPT-2 sparse autoencoders (dead fraction 0.991 at eight times the unaltered offset, most of those units alive at the start so they died *during* training). The step size - learning rate times fan-in, derivable from Adam's per-weight cap and the measured alignment of early weight updates with inputs - is the weak factor: an image-classifier exponent of 1.158 for a constant live-unit count is qualified down to 0.923 with the largest fan-in removed, and the sparse-autoencoder pre-registered test **failed at 0.670** before a design defect (unrotated full-size inputs left in GPT-2's own coordinates) was fixed and gave 0.971. The author also states that a second published mechanism predicts the same FC1-versus-FC2 ordering and that this measurement cannot separate them. Mechanical rule for us: **where two factors are offered for one outcome, each gets its own evidence statement, its own pre-registered test and its own failure record, and any alternative mechanism that predicts the same observation is named beside ours - because an ordering reproduced by two different causes is not evidence for either.** Cross-reference run 697 (the held-out adjudication set the statistical pass never saw) and run 693 (intervals frozen at the search root, the same uncertainty about which factor did the work).

- **Done:** arxiv-2611.25165 - Separating Interpretability and Reconstruction in Sparse Autoencoders (read full text v2: abstract, §1-§6, supplement metric definitions, Tables 1-2 and referenced supplementary tables as cells; figures 1-19 captions only, not inspected as images)
- **Pattern:** **A decomposition's leftover should be named, not hidden inside mixed units - and this paper is the cleanest worked example of that move because it makes the discarded component deliberately uninterpretable.** The sparse branch is allowed to be an incomplete reconstruction; the residual branch exists to absorb the rest so the sparse code is never pressured to encode non-semantic variance, and the L2 loss is applied to the sum of the two rather than to the sparse branch alone. That design makes the incompleteness of the explanation explicit and auditable, and it is the structural opposite of what the head's absorption and polysemanticity entries describe, where a single dictionary quietly spends latents on reconstruction-only directions. Mechanical rule for us: **every white-box decomposition we publish names the variance it has declared out of scope and reports the read-out for that declared-out component separately - because a component that is only implicitly discarded is indistinguishable from one that was never found.** Cross-reference run 699 (dictionary latents positioned by chat-format boilerplate rather than meaning - the same non-semantic variance, unaccounted for) and run 696 (the ranking that is worse than a shuffled null once a voxel of structure is preserved).
- **Pattern:** **The control that separates "this component improved" from "this component got more capacity" is to freeze it, let the second component absorb the remainder, and check whether the read-out moved - and this paper runs exactly that pair.** The sequential baseline (pretrained SAE frozen, residual branch trained alone) lifts reconstruction by 15.4-24.8% with interpretability *unchanged*, so the residual branch alone buys fidelity and nothing else; only joint training, with the decorrelation penalty that breaks the identity-map shortcut, buys 10.5-43.3% MonoScore on comparable R². The parameter-matched widened-SAE table rules out raw capacity as the explanation across 18 paired rows. This is the head's recurring "a gain needs a null that spends the same budget" lesson in its cheapest concrete form. Mechanical rule for us: **any claim that an added component improves a read-out is paired with (i) a frozen-component variant that spends the same extra parameters and (ii) a parameter-matched wider baseline, because both are one training run each and together they turn a plausible gain into an attributable one.** Cross-reference run 695 (the construction baseline that made a score interpretable), run 697 (the held-out adjudication set the statistical pass never saw).
- **Pattern:** **A coherence score computed in the same space the model was fit to compress is inflated by that model's own geometry, and this paper concedes the point and answers it with a label-only metric.** MonoScore is an activation-weighted mean pairwise cosine between strongly co-activating inputs, and the authors deliberately compute the similarity with the encoder being decomposed rather than an independent one - justified as removing a second model, but it removes the check that a latent's coherence is not an artefact of that embedding geometry; they therefore also report binary purity, which uses only ground-truth labels and no encoder, and note their absolute MonoScore values are not comparable to the original paper's. Compare run 698 (agreement between two read-outs of one forward pass cannot separate representation from input-side structure) and run 696 (activation-only attributions scoring below a shuffled null). Mechanical rule for us: **every coherence, purity or structure score we report is accompanied by a measure that never touches the representation the model was optimised to reproduce - labels, held-out behaviour, or an independent encoder - because a metric defined in the fitted space will report the fit, not the concept.**

- **Done:** goodfire-com-research-mapping-latent-spaces-llama - Mapping the Latent Space of Llama 3.3 70B (read full blog post, every section: feature map, feature examples, steering, methods, moderation, limitations, references; the interactive UMAP explorer and the steering plots were not inspected beyond the axes and numbers stated in prose; no figures read directly)
- **Pattern:** **A model-judged behaviour score is not monotone with the capability the behaviour borrows from, and this post is the cheapest worked example in the head - because the authors report the failure against themselves.** Their steering sweep has the judge scoring coherence and pirate-speak on a 0-100 scale; the score rises sharply at mid-strength, but factual degradation begins at a *lower* strength than the behaviour change, and at the top of the range responses the authors describe as factually wrong are still scored well (magnitudes restated in nautical units). The post names it as a pitfall of LLM-judged steering evaluation rather than hiding it, which is why it is citable. Compare run 698 (behavioural-versus-internal agreement as the cheap sanity check) and run 697 (a composite benchmark hiding which component broke): all three are the same shape - one readout standing in for a multi-part effect. Mechanical rule for us: **every steering or intervention result is reported with at least one metric the judge was never asked about, the sweep is reported as a curve rather than a peak, and a high judge score in a region the authors or we have called broken is treated as evidence about the evaluation's narrowness - because here the best-scoring region is the most wrong one.** Cross-reference run 697 (GSM8K accuracy grading arithmetic), run 80 in this folder (the steering sweet spot and off-target effects, the same non-monotonicity measured behaviourally).
- **Pattern:** **Dictionary latents can be positioned by the *formatting* of the data rather than by meaning, and the diagnostic is geometric and free.** Features for special tokens and for strings present in every conversation (the knowledge-cutoff date is the example) appear as isolated points and small islands away from the central mass of the UMAP map; the authors give two candidate causes - very large magnitudes for special tokens, and memorisation pressure from frequent repetition - and cite the superposition-and-memorisation work for the second. Either way the cluster position is a property of the chat-formatted training corpus, not of the concept, and the post is honest that apparent behaviour-clusters were never verified to have distinct effects. Mechanical rule for us: **before interpreting any latent on chat-formatted data, check whether its top activating contexts are a formatting token or a boilerplate string, and report the map-position outlier status of every feature we build an argument on - because a feature that only fires on the system-prompt boilerplate can look like a clean concept cluster and be nothing but a data-format fingerprint.** Cross-reference run 698 (per-position readings whose movement may be input-side structure), run 696 (activation-only attributions scoring *below* a shuffled null - the same lesson that a plausible-looking feature can carry no mechanism).

## Run 698 (2026-11-24)

- Status 2026-11-24 (run 698): started clean - `git pull --rebase -q` left HEAD at `367fb246` ("literature (aether): entry 697 goodfire-com-research-understanding-memorization-via-loss-curvature"), so run 697's single entry commit landed and nothing was inherited (`git status --short` empty before any write; `grep -c '^- \[ \]'` on the Priority block = **0**, still no Priority block). Counts **measured** from the repo root with the anchored filter (`ls -1 docs/literature/aether/*.md | grep -v -E '/(INDEX|NOTES|SEED_QUEUE)\\.md$' | wc -l`): entry files **697 -> 698**; `wc -l INDEX.md` **793 -> 794**, appended by `/tmp/lit/app_stories.py`, which reads the line from `/tmp/lit/idx_stories.txt` (built with `write_file`, whitespace-collapsed in Python) and asserts `line.startswith(key + ' | ')`, no embedded newline, `key not in s` before, `s.endswith('\\n')`, `s2.startswith(s)`, `not s2.endswith('\\n\\n')`, `s2.count('\\n')==n0+1`, then re-reads and checks `count(key)` - returned **1** (`ok lines 793 -> 794 keycount 1`). **698 mod 3 = 2, so THIS RUN IS SINGLE**; the **NEXT run is the BATCH at file count 699** (`699 mod 3 = 0`) and must carry **both** labels as two commits (`literature (aether): entry 699 <key>` then `literature (aether): 699 entries`), with `git log --oneline --all | grep -c "699 entries"` checked **before** writing the batch label.
- **Done:** goodfire-com-research-stories-in-space - Meandering on Manifolds: The Neural Geometry of Stories Over Time (read full blog post, all sections; figures and interactive demos not inspected; the linked arXiv paper was NOT read, and the entry says so)
- **Pattern:** **A per-position reading is a trajectory, not a point, and this is the first entry in the head whose unit is the *path* through activation space rather than a snapshot.** Its whole method is one forward pass over a story with something harvested at every sentence boundary - a behavioural six-emotion readout from prompting and a last-token activation - so the object of study is a sequence of states and the claim is about how the sequence moves, not about where a concept sits. That inverts the default in this folder, where a probe is fitted once, read at one position per input (usually the final token, sometimes an answer position) and averaged over a dataset; an average over positions destroys exactly the dynamic this post reports. Mechanical rule for us: **every probe we fit is also run densely over positions on long inputs and reported as a per-input path before it is averaged, and where we publish a single number it is labelled as a statistic over a path rather than as a state - because a mean over token positions is a statement about the corpus, not about the model's state at any moment.** Cross-reference run 697 (the same "a statistic of an average is not the average of the statistic" failure at the loss level) and run 696 (a per-unit worth is a property of the ablated set, the same level-of-aggregation error on units).
- **Pattern:** **The cheapest causal-free sanity check in this literature is to measure an observable and an internal quantity on the same forward passes and see whether they move together - and this post shows both the value and the trap of it.** They read the model's emotion verbally and its activation geometrically at the same sentence boundaries, so the two series are aligned by construction and the comparison needs no retraining; this is cheaper than an intervention and it catches gross misalignment (a manifold that does not move when the story turns). The trap is that both readings are downstream of the same input, so agreement is consistent with a shared surface feature as much as with a shared internal cause, and the post's own defence is only that separate internal probes are accurate. Mechanical rule for us: **a behavioural-vs-internal agreement plot is the first figure in any entry that claims a concept is represented, it is paired with at least one intervention that bends one series and is checked against the other, and agreement alone is never written up as evidence of a mechanism - because a correlation between two readouts of one model cannot separate representation from input-side structure.** Cross-reference run 697 (the held-out adjudication set the statistical pass never saw), run 695 (the construction baseline that made a score interpretable).

- **Done:** goodfire-com-research-understanding-memorization-via-loss-curvature - Understanding Memorization via Loss Curvature (read full blog post, all sections; figures not inspected; the linked arXiv paper was NOT read, and the entry says so)
- **Pattern:** **This is the first entry in the head to give weight space a *basis whose axes have an operational meaning*, and the meaning is a prediction about intervention - which is the shape our white-box model needs.** K-FAC curvature eigenvectors over each weight matrix are re-expressed as widely-used versus narrowly-used components, the low-curvature end is zeroed, and the basis is computed once from unlabelled text with no trainable mask. Two features make it generalisable to us: the axis semantics are stated in advance of the test ("directions many examples depend on" should be the ones whose removal hurts broadly), and the adjudication is a held-out set the statistical pass never saw - memorised quotations versus fitted Dolma sequences - so a method that only hits its own fitting data is distinguishable from one that found a mechanism. Mechanical rule for us: **any weight-derived object we publish is a basis with a stated per-axis intervention prediction and a held-out probe set that the basis was not computed on; a decomposition graded only on the data it was fitted to does not earn a mechanism claim.**
- **Pattern:** **Sharpness flips sign between the per-example and the dataset level, and this paper is the head's cleanest statement that a statistic of an average is not the average of the statistic.** Prior work finds memorised examples sit in sharp regions; averaged over a corpus, the same quantity inverts, because a direction only one example uses barely moves the mean while attention and composition directions move it a lot. That is the same class of error as run 696's ablation result (a per-unit worth is not a property of the unit) and run 693's frozen intervals (a per-seed reading is not a per-hypothesis one), now at the level of the loss itself. Mechanical rule for us: **every statistic we report is labelled with the level it was computed at - per-input, per-batch, per-dataset - and where we compute both, we report both, because this literature repeatedly draws a qualitative conclusion from whichever level happened to be convenient.**
- **Pattern:** **A composite benchmark score can hide which component actually broke, and the paper's worked example is the diagnosis pattern worth copying.** GSM8K accuracy drops, and the post-level inspection shows the reasoning chain intact with the error confined to the final arithmetic step, so a benchmark named for reasoning was grading arithmetic. Combined with the spectrum result (pure memorisation 3-16% of original accuracy, reasoning robust or slightly better, arithmetic and factual recall in between), the lesson is that an aggregate is only as informative as the sub-capability it is most sensitive to. Mechanical rule for us: **when we report a drop under an intervention, we decompose it to the step or sub-task that moved and name the benchmark's blind spot - and when removal *improves* a metric (their ViT validation went 67% to 71.7% after deleting structure), we treat the improvement as a finding about the model, not as noise.**
- **Could not read:** nothing failed twice and nothing was abandoned. **Two candidates were retired on discovery, both by `ls` on the two folders, not by failure**: `transformer-circuits-pub-2021-garcon-index-html` already exists (fetched 13,114 chars before the check - the fetch was wasted, so **run the `ls` check before the fetch**, this run did it after for one item), and the `2023-july-update` / `2023-may-update` pair plus every other transformer-circuits update in the queue is already present. **Discovery, and a correction to the reference count.** `web_search` was **not** called (run 662 recorded `daily_cap_reached`). The queue was re-parsed with the unmutated single-line `- ` test over 149 rows; a slug-key derivation gave **124 unread**, but that number is **inflated and not to be trusted**: the derived `slug-<host>-<path>` keys do not match the folder's naming convention (the folder uses `goodfire-com-research-...`-style keys without the `www-`/`slug-` prefixes), so every unread test was re-run by **substring** against the real on-disk key list, which is the only reliable form. Under substring testing the entire `anthropic.com/research/` block I sampled (11 slugs, sleeper-agents through tracing-thoughts) and the transformer-circuits update/personal-post block (garcon, july/may updates) are **already read**; genuinely free Goodfire research posts include `belief-dynamics-icl-steering`, `stories-in-space`, `open-problems-in-mech-interp`, `the-circuits-research-landscape`, `why-larger-models-learn-more`, `a-geometric-calculator`, `forking-fast`. **Standing correction for the next run: compute unread with `grep -q -- "<slug fragment>"` against `ls docs/literature/{aether,core}/*.md`, never with a derived slug key**, because the derived form makes the queue look 100+ items deep when it is not.

- Status 2026-11-23 (run 696): started clean - `git pull --rebase -q` left HEAD at the run-695 entry commit (`1aa406d1`, "literature (aether): entry 695 arxiv-2610.37676"), so run 695's entry commit and push both landed and nothing was inherited (`git status --short` empty before any write). - **Done:** arxiv-2611.23112 - Large-scale Neuron Community Discovery Reveals Neuron-Sparse Behaviors in Language Models (read full text v1, Sections 1-8 plus Appendices A-F; figures not inspected)
- **Pattern:** **Ten verification entries and five interpretability entries asked for the same thing - a per-unit reading that is printed rather than executed and discarded - and this is the first one that prints a *schedule of per-unit arrays at scale*, on the interpretability side, and it shows the array worth printing is the group membership, not the individual score.** One forward and one backward pass per prompt produce an attribution for every MLP neuron; the top five per layer become a sparse set; a popularity-corrected co-occurrence z-score becomes a graph; six Leiden resolutions become partitions; every surviving group is then certified per prompt by three KL divergences. Nothing in that chain needs a solve, and the published artefacts are the group list and a per-group behavioural label. The load-bearing measurement is the ablation, not the ranking: the activation-times-gradient score covers 9.94%, gradients alone 6.31%, and **every activation-only score (2.5-2.7%) is beaten by the shuffled-community null (2.91%)** - so a per-unit ranking is validated by the causal test it feeds, and a ranking that predicts nothing can be worse than a random one that preserves the group's shape. Mechanical rule for us: **we publish per-unit readings as group-level objects with a membership array, a per-prompt certificate vector and a shuffled-null coverage curve beside them, and we never report a per-unit score's usefulness from its correlation with anything other than the intervention it is supposed to predict - because here the honest ranking is little better than a shuffled one once a single voxel of structure (the group's layer set and size) is preserved.** Cross-reference run 693 (the per-unit case map computed and unprinted), run 690 (the reachable-set generator list unread), run 694 (the one-forward-pass screen as the first column), run 695 (the construction baseline that makes a score interpretable).
- **Pattern:** **A group's necessity does not distribute over its members, and this is the first entry in the head to measure the failure with a complete subset sweep - which invalidates the way every attribution paper in this folder reads a per-unit score.** All 64 subsets of the six-neuron `an`-vowel community are ablated across 100 prompts: the best single member alone nearly reproduces the behaviour (0.68 against 0.06 for the full set and 4.56 for none), yet removing any single member of the full set leaves KL at 0.06-0.07, and a pair that excludes the strongest member reaches about 0.2. So the strongest individual reading is redundant inside the group, and the same protocol on the in-context copying community finds a single member whose removal takes KL from 0.05 to 7.37 - i.e. the relationship between member and group ranges over both extremes and cannot be assumed from the individual score. Compare the head's verification entries, which report a per-neuron multiplier or split score as if it were a per-neuron worth (runs 678, 682, 688, 691): a worth is a property of the set you ablate, not of the unit. Mechanical rule for us: **any per-unit score we publish is accompanied by at least the single-unit and leave-one-out interventions, and where we can afford it the full power set on the small groups (2^6 here), because one unit's isolated effect and its marginal effect inside the group are different quantities that this literature routinely conflates.** Cross-reference run 694 (read-direction and write-direction specificity as an independent pair, the same "one number is not enough" finding on an SAE), run 688 (the split multiplier as an optimised per-neuron worth), run 149/150 in this folder (a descriptor graded by the rank it gives the true cause among matched alternatives).
- **Pattern:** **Coverage is a property of the discovery pipeline, not of the model, and this paper says so in the same sentence that reports the number - which makes it the cleanest model in the head for how to report a partial explanation's reach.** 10.3% of held-out prompts at one layer, 8.7% at four, 3.3% at twelve, 6.7% at one layer on a 16-neuron budget; the authors state explicitly that missed mechanisms are not absent mechanisms and that the figure is an evaluation-distribution property of *this* pipeline, then support the claim with a mechanism-level audit - attributions taken only at the final token, disjoint partitions forcing each neuron into one group, and a resolution sweep where six of fourteen thresholds retain 99.1% of the union yet the best single threshold reaches only 5.74%. The taxonomy is offered as illustration, not measurement, because an LLM wrote the labels. Compare run 695, which turned a confounded score into a reportable finding by adding a construction baseline, and run 690, where a granularity dial had to be reported at three settings. Mechanical rule for us: **every recall or coverage number we publish is prefixed with the pipeline that produced it, followed by the null and the granularity sweep, and suffixed with the negative statement it does not support - because a partial dictionary's reach invites exactly one overclaim, and the fix is not a stronger method but a longer sentence.** Cross-reference run 695 (the explanation-free rule beside every score), run 693 (intervals frozen at the search root), run 145 (the redirect-stub and slug-dedupe lesson that this run's retired candidates repeated).
